the full E-determinant of B, of signature (1, 3), which is supplied by Lemma 3.3.
     It remains to consider the six-dimensional higher centers. Here B = A, so cγ = 0. For
(f, a) = (2, 3), the two holomorphic multiplicities above one E-embedding sum to three. The
alternatives (0, 3) and (3, 0) make every column pure and the factor CM. Otherwise the vector is
(1, 2), up to order, with imbalance (−1, 1). Equation (3.4) gives n1 = n2 .
     For (f, a) = (3, 2), the three multiplicities belong to {0, 1, 2} and sum to three. The vector
(1, 1, 1) is excluded by the rank-two argument in Lemma 3.7. The remaining vector is a permutation
of (0, 1, 2), with imbalance a permutation of (−2, 0, 2). The Galois action over E on the three
embeddings of K/E is transitive and therefore contains a three-cycle. Order the columns so that
the imbalance is (−2, 0, 2). Applying (3.4) to the three cyclic conjugates gives, up to reversing their
order,
                          −n1 + n3 = 0,        −n2 + n1 = 0,         −n3 + n2 = 0.
Thus n1 = n2 = n3 .
    In either case, after opposite pairs have been supplied, only a power of fj=1 D(Wj ), or its
                                                                               N

opposite, remains. The center projectors are admissible algebraic degree-one maps. Alternation and
those projectors identify the basic product fj=1 D(Wj ) nontrivially with the determinant of the
                                            N

full E-embedding space of A. The given hyperbolic form supplies this determinant by Lemma 3.3.
    These cases exhaust Lemma 3.7. The equalities above concern arbitrary integer net exponents, so
they verify determinant supply in every tensor degree. Products, opposite pairs, and the direct-sum
maps preserve CM supply. Assumption (d) of Theorem 2.5 is therefore satisfied, and that theorem
proves the result on every self-power.



                                                   23
4     The geometric seed for the Weil-plane theorem
We begin the proof of Theorem 3.1, the algebraicity input used in Lemma 3.3. We follow Markman’s
secant-sheaf and spin construction [25, 26]. The argument will retain the actual polarized Hermitian
space and the full derived obstruction group throughout descent and deformation.
    We first construct a coherent perfect sheaf on the product of a genus-three Jacobian and its
dual, with a coherent action of a finite group of translations combined with line tensors. An exact
Hochschild–Atiyah trace identity and a dimension calculation then show that the semiregularity
map is injective on the entire invariant derived Ext2 group. A separate finite Clifford calculation
identifies the normalized rational Betti character of the same sheaf: it has a nonzero Weil component,
and all its components remain Hodge on the full compatible marked domain.
    We next descend the sheaf to a twisted sheaf on the finite quotient. A projective-bundle
construction turns it into an ordinary coherent sheaf while retaining the full obstruction group.
We prove the restricted smooth-projective-germ semiregularity theorem needed to deform that
ordinary sheaf, and recover the normalized character by algebraic projective-bundle operations.
Finally, proper Hilbert parameter spaces and the Baire theorem extend the local algebraicity to
every marked period; rational isogenies pass from the seed lattice to every hyperbolic quadratic
lattice. The proof of Theorem 3.1 is completed in Subsection 8.4.
    The geometric input is a pair of finite orbits of Abel curves on a genus-three Jacobian. We
choose the orbits so that their ideals have no sections after any degree-zero twist of twice the
principal polarization. This vanishing will control the fiber cohomology of a fixed Fourier–Mukai
transform and allow its shifted derived dual to be an ordinary coherent sheaf. We construct that
sheaf, its coherent line-twisted translation action, and the finite quotient used in the deformation
argument. The source ideal and transform are those of Markman [25, (6.2.1), (6.2.3), and (9.2.1)];
the compact-type degeneration below supplies the required orbits.
    All varieties in this section are over C. If V is a group variety, tv (z) = z + v, and tv D denotes
the image translate of a divisor D. Thus t∗v O(D) = O(t−v D).

For clarity, these normalizations follow directly from the Jacobian lattice. In the analytic description

principal class is 3i=1 ei ∧ fi . Pairing Θ2 /2 with each basis element of Λ2 H 1 (X, Z) gives the same

For a translate of Σp the analogous map is the negative of this isomorphism.
    We will repeatedly use curve Riemann–Roch and duality in the following form. For a proper
Gorenstein curve D and an invertible sheaf N ,
               χ(D, N ) = deg N + χ(D, OD ),           H 1 (D, N )∨ = H 0 (D, ωD ⊗ N −1 ).            (4.8)
The numerical Riemann–Roch formula is the rank-one case of [39, Lemma 53.5.2, Tag 0BS6]; the
canonical duality isomorphism is stated in [39, Remark 53.4.3, Tag 0BS4]. We will apply them to
smooth curves.
    The temporary restriction d ≥ 3 loses no imaginary quadratic field: the final proof of Theorem 3.1
replaces a purely imaginary generator u0 by u = 2u0 , scaling the associated Hermitian form without
changing its isotropic subspaces.
Proposition 4.1 (Simultaneous equivariant seeds). For every integer d ≥ 3, put n = d + 1. There
exist a smooth nonhyperelliptic genus-three curve C, a point p ∈ C, and cyclic subgroups G1 , G2 ⊂
X = Pic2 (C) of order n, with G1 ∩ G2 = 0, as follows. There are cosets {s1 , . . . , sn } = s0 + G2 and
{t1 , . . . , tn } = t0 + G1 such that

                    Ci = tsi Cp ,    Σj = ttj Σp ,          A=         Ci ,     Σ=         Σj         (4.9)
                                                                 i=1                 j=1


                                                      25
are disjoint within each of the two indicated unions, and

              H 0 (X, IA ⊗ H ⊗2 ⊗ N ) = H 0 (X, IΣ ⊗ H ⊗2 ⊗ N ) = 0          for every N ∈ X.
                                                                                           b      (4.10)

    The uniform vanishing in the proposition has a specific role in the sheaf construction. For every
x ∈ X, the product ideal IA It−x Σ is contained in IA , so every degree-zero twist of this ideal by H ⊗2
has zero H 0 . Its quotient in OX has one-dimensional support; the ideal sequence and ample-line
vanishing will then give zero H 3 . For general x, the two orbit unions are disjoint. The restriction of
H ⊗2 to each component has degree six, greater than 2g(C) − 2 = 4, which also gives zero H 2 for
these parameters.
    We will prove that these cohomology groups are the derived fibers of the fixed transform. The
first two vanishings give a local model K 1 → K 2 , and the generic H 2 -vanishing makes its dual
differential generically injective. Integrality of the parameter space then makes that dual differential
injective everywhere. Its cokernel is the coherent sheaf we seek. We first construct the orbits
required by the proposition.

4.2     Constructing the seed by a compact-type degeneration
On a product of three elliptic curves, the central support of the limiting Abel curve will be the
union of the three coordinate axes. The next two lemmas show that four suitably translated copies
of this star impose more vanishing conditions than a nonzero section of a product of degree-two
lines can satisfy. We will carry this vanishing to a smooth Jacobian through a polarized family and
the proper relative Picard scheme.

4.2.1    Vanishing on the elliptic star
Lemma 4.2 (Singular marked points of a trilinear form). Let V1 , V2 , V3 be two-dimensional vector
spaces and let 0 ̸= T ∈ V1 ⊗ V2 ⊗ V3 . Suppose a finite indexed collection

                                (xh , yh , zh ) ∈ P(V1∗ ) × P(V2∗ ) × P(V3∗ )

has at most two indices in every fiber of each coordinate projection. At most three indices satisfy all
three equations
                          T (−, yh , zh ) = T (xh , −, zh ) = T (xh , yh , −) = 0.              (4.11)
Indices, rather than distinct triples, are counted.
Proof. Choose bases and write T (x, y, z) = y t M (x)z, where M (x) = x0 A + x1 B is a pencil of
two-by-two matrices. Put D(x) = det M (x). Equations (4.11) imply

                        M (x)z = 0,        y t M (x) = 0,       y t Az = y t Bz = 0.              (4.12)

Rank two for M (x) is impossible. If its rank is one, its adjugate is a nonzero multiple of zy t , so the
determinant derivative formula and (4.12) give ∂x0 D(x) = ∂x1 D(x) = 0. If M (x) = 0, its adjugate
is zero and the same conclusion holds. For a nonzero binary quadratic D, the gradient is a nonzero
linear map in characteristic zero and has at most one projective zero. All qualifying indices then
have the same x, and there are at most two.
    It remains to consider D ≡ 0. Every nonzero matrix in the pencil has rank one. A one-
dimensional pencil makes T a product of three linear forms. For a two-dimensional pencil write
A = uv t and B = u′ v ′t . The equality

                                 0 = det(A + B) = det[u, u′ ] det[v, v ′ ]

                                                     26
shows that the pencil has a common left or right factor. After permuting the variables, both cases
have the form T = l(x)q(y, z). If the bilinear form q has rank two, the last two partial contractions
force l(x) = 0, again allowing at most two indices. If q has rank one, write T = l(x)m(y)k(z).
Equations (4.11) then hold precisely when at least two of l(x), m(y), k(z) vanish. Each of the three
distinguished projective coordinate values occurs at most twice. If N indices qualify, counting these
incidences gives 2N ≤ 6, hence N ≤ 3.

Lemma 4.3 (The elliptic star). Let J0 = E1 × E2 × E3 be a product of elliptic curves with origins,
and let Z0 be the reduced union of the three coordinate elliptic axes through zero. Let K ⊂ J0 be
a finite subgroup of order at least four whose projection to every Ei is injective. The translates
Z(g) = g + Z0 , g ∈ K, are pairwise disjoint. For line bundles Di of degree two on Ei ,
                                                                          
                             H 0 J0 , IS          Z(g) ⊗ (D1 ⊠ D2 ⊠ D3 ) = 0.                      (4.13)
                                            g∈K


The same vanishing holds for any closed subscheme with that support.

Proof. If axis i at g met axis j at h ̸= g, the coordinates of g and h outside {i, j} would agree.
There is at least one such coordinate, contradicting injectivity there. This proves disjointness.
    Put Vi = H 0 (Ei , Di ). Formula (4.8) gives dim Vi = 2 and h0 (Di (−q)) = 1 for every q ∈ Ei .
Evaluation is therefore basepoint-free and defines a degree-two map φi : Ei → P(Vi∗ ), each of whose
fibers has at most two distinct points. The marks
                                                       
                            φ1 (g1 ), φ2 (g2 ), φ3 (g3 ) ,    g = (g1 , g2 , g3 ) ∈ K,

have at most two indices in each coordinate fiber, since K → Ei is injective. The product formula
for sections identifies a section of D1 ⊠ D2 ⊠ D3 with a tensor in V1 ⊗ V2 ⊗ V3 . Its restriction to
axis i at g is contraction by evaluation at the other two coordinates. Vanishing on Z(g) is exactly
(4.11). Lemma 4.2 forbids this for four or more indices for a nonzero section. Finally, the ideal of a
closed subscheme with this support is contained in the radical ideal of the reduced union, which
proves the last assertion.

4.2.2   Finite cohomology models and base change
We need to pass from vanishing on the limiting product to vanishing on nearby Jacobians, uniformly
over their Picard twists. Later we will identify every derived fiber of the fixed Fourier–Mukai
kernel. The following finite-model statement supplies both semicontinuity for the first step and
arbitrary base change for the second. It also defines the determinant line that identifies the limiting
polarization.

Lemma 4.4 (Finite models and arbitrary base change). Let B be a regular Noetherian scheme
of finite dimension, let f : V → B be projective, and let F be a coherent sheaf flat over B. Then
Rf∗ F is locally represented by a bounded complex K • of finite-rank locally free sheaves. For every
morphism h : T → B there is a derived base-change isomorphism

                                       Lh∗ Rf∗ F ≃ RfT,∗ (F|V ×B T ),                              (4.14)

where the restriction on the right is ordinary, and the left side is represented locally by K • ⊗ OT . The
functions b 7→ dimκ(b) H i (Vb , Fb ) are upper semicontinuous. If all derived fibers have cohomology in
degrees [c, e], the local model can be chosen in precisely those degrees.


                                                         27
Proof. Over an affine U = Spec R ⊂ B, choose a finite affine cover Vi of VU . All finite intersections
are affine because VU is separated. Its Čech complex has terms
                                   q
                                            Y
                                  CR =                Γ(Vi0 ∩ · · · ∩ Viq , F).                  (4.15)
                                         i0 <···<iq

It is bounded. Each term is R-flat: for a module on an affine scheme, flatness over R is checked at
the total-space stalks, where it is the assumed relative flatness. For any R-algebra R′ , CR• ⊗ R′ is
                                                                                                R
                                                                               ′
exactly the affine Čech complex of the ordinary pullback of F to VU ×R R . A bounded complex
of flat modules is K-flat, so this tensor product also computes the derived tensor product. Affine
Čech cohomology therefore proves (4.14) for affine base changes, and those isomorphisms glue for
an arbitrary T .
    The cohomology modules of CR     • are finite over R, by proper coherence for a projective scheme

over a Noetherian affine base [39, Lemma 30.16.1(3), Tag 0B5T]. A finite module over the regular
Noetherian finite-dimensional ring R has a finite resolution by finite projective modules [39, Section
10.110, Tag 065U]. The truncation triangles, applied to the finitely many cohomology modules of CR   •,

consequently make its derived object perfect. After localizing, it has the stated finite free model.
     For such a model the dimension of the i-th fiber cohomology is

                           rank K i − rank(di ⊗ κ(b)) − rank(di−1 ⊗ κ(b)).                       (4.16)

Ranks of matrices are lower semicontinuous by their minors; (4.16) proves upper semicontinuity.
Finally, at a term below c, the first remaining differential is injective on the fiber. Invert a maximal
minor, split off this term, and replace the next term by its locally free cokernel. At a term above e,
do the dual operation using a fiberwise surjective last differential. Repeating at a chosen point and
shrinking its neighborhood gives a model in [c, e].

    We also use the perfect determinant in a concrete form. For a finite locally free complex put
                          i
det K • = i (det K i )(−1) . The determinant of an acyclic complex is canonically trivialized by
          N

the exact sequences of its cycles. Applying these trivializations to cones makes the determinants
independent of the finite model, so they glue and commute with base change. In particular, a model
K 0 → K 1 of equal ranks has a regular determinant section of (det K • )−1 ; its zero set is where the
fiber complex is not acyclic.

4.2.3   From the polarized star to a smooth seed
Proof of Proposition 4.1. The polarized elliptic limit. We first realize the elliptic product with its
product principal polarization as the central Jacobian of a smoothing. This identifies the central
twice-theta line with the degree-two product lines tested in Lemma 4.3.
   Start with the stable compact-type curve

                                C0 = R ∪ E1 ∪ E2 ∪ E3 ,               R ≃ P1 ,                   (4.17)

where the elliptic tails Ei meet R at three distinct nodes ei and have no other intersections. We
choose a projective one-parameter smoothing C → S over a smooth pointed complex algebraic curve
(S, 0) with regular total surface and with smooth nonhyperelliptic noncentral fibers.
    Here is the existence check, for the unmarked stable curve. The local presentation of ΩC0 at a
node has length one, its local Ext1 is supported at the nodes, and the local-to-global sequence gives
Ext2 (ΩC0 , OC0 ) = 0. The global tangent contribution is the sum of the H 1 of the tangent lines on the
normalized components, with their node points removed. It is zero for TR (−e1 − e2 − e3 ) = OP1 (−1),

                                                        28
and has dimension one for each TEi (−ei ) = OEi (−ei ). The three node-smoothing parameters add
three more dimensions. Thus the unmarked versal germ is smooth of dimension six. The algebraic
input is the smooth Deligne–Mumford stable-curve stack and density of its smooth-curve locus
[39, Theorem 109.25.3, Tag 0E99]; it gives an algebraic versal chart here. Smooth hyperelliptic
genus-three curves have a five-dimensional locus, as seen from eight branch points on P1 modulo
PGL2 . Its closure is proper in that chart. A general algebraic curve through the chosen point
is not contained in this closure and has nonzero first derivative in each node parameter. The
local total-space equation at each node is then uv = t times a unit, so the total surface is regular.
Shrinking S gives the asserted noncentral fibers. Choose a smooth point on R only now. Smoothness
near it lifts it, after an étale localization of S, to a section σ. This point was not included in the
six-dimensional unmarked germ.
    Let J be the componentwise degree-zero relative Picard space of C/S. The exact Picard input
used here is [13, Section 2, p. 136]: for a stable family this open group space is separated, smooth,
and of finite type, and its fibers are semiabelian. The normalization sequence for a connected nodal
curve with dual graph ∆ gives
                                                           Y
                                b1 (∆)
                          1 −→ Gm      −→ Pic0 (C0 ) −→         Pic0 (Cv ) −→ 1.                   (4.18)
                                                            v

Indeed, its kernel is the set of gluing scalars at the nodes modulo rescaling on each component;
one scalar remains per graph cycle. Our graph is a tree and Pic0 (R) = 0, so J0 = i Pic0 (Ei ).
                                                                                             Q

All noncentral fibers are smooth Jacobians. Thus every geometric fiber is proper and connected.
Deligne’s [13, Section 1.1, pp. 131–132] states both that a separated flat finite-presentation space
with proper connected geometric fibers is proper, and that a generic polarization on an abelian
space over a normal integral base extends uniquely and makes that space projective. Consequently
J is an abelian scheme and the generic canonical polarization extends to λJ : J → Jb. Its degree is
locally constant and is one generically, so it is principal. We use only the componentwise degree-zero
Picard space; no separatedness of the unrestricted relative Picard functor is asserted.
    The decomposition of the central Jacobian as an abelian variety does not yet identify its
polarization. To obtain the product polarization needed for the vanishing lemma, we compute a
theta line by a determinant of cohomology. After an étale localization, the relative Picard space has
a universal line bundle; this is the precise universal-line input from [11, Section 2.1, last paragraph].
Restrict it to C ×S J and rigidify it along σ; denote it by U. With pC the projection to J , put
                                                                 −1
                                  T = det RpC,∗ U ⊗ OC (2σ)             .                          (4.19)

The base J is regular, and this is a flat projective curve family with a line bundle. Lemma 4.4 and
the determinant construction following it define (4.19) and its base changes. Every fiber has Euler
characteristic zero and cohomology in degrees zero and one; locally it has a two-term equal-rank
model, with determinant section. On a smooth nonhyperelliptic fiber the section vanishes exactly
on the effective degree-two locus W2 . Its generic multiplicity there is one: at an effective degree-two
line the groups H 0 and H 1 have dimension one, and the first-order differential is cup product

                                 H 1 (OC ) −→ Hom(H 0 (N ), H 1 (N )).

Its dual is multiplication H 0 (N ) ⊗ H 0 (ωC N −1 ) → H 0 (ωC ), which is nonzero on nonzero sections.
Splitting the invertible blocks of a local two-term model shows that this nonzero first-order differential
is the linear term of its remaining one-by-one determinant. Thus the determinant section has a
simple zero there. It follows that the homomorphism ϕT associated to T is the generic canonical

polarization. It equals λJ over S, since the two morphisms agree on the schematically dense generic
fiber and the target is separated.

Every line in Pic0 (J0 ) is the external product of three degree-zero lines.
     The flat Abel limit and the torsion orbits. We next construct a flat family of Abel images whose
central support is the elliptic star. Support is enough for the vanishing lemma, since the ideal of
any thickening of that star is contained in its reduced ideal.
    The pointed Abel map on the smooth fibers, q 7→ O(q − σ), extends to a morphism α : C → J .
We give the extension argument to record the support of its limit. The map is a rational S-map,
defined on the smooth-fiber open. Elimination of indeterminacy for a regular surface and a proper
target [39, Lemma 54.4.3, Tag 0C5H] gives a sequence of blowups at closed points of the central
fiber on which it becomes a morphism. Each reduced fiber of this sequence over an indeterminacy
point is a connected tree of rational curves. Every morphism P1 → J0 is constant: pullback of
the translation-invariant one-forms is zero, and those forms span every cotangent space, so the
differential is zero in characteristic zero. The resolved map is therefore constant on each such tree.
Its schematic graph image in C ×S J is integral, proper and birational over C, with one underlying
point in each fiber. A proper morphism with finite fibers is finite [39, Lemma 37.44.1, Tag 02LS]. A
finite integral birational morphism to the normal surface C is an isomorphism, by integral closedness
in its function field. This descends the resolved map to α. The relative first Abel morphism and its
tail twists are also given by [11, Section 2.2, (7), (9), and Proposition 2.4] for a regular smoothing
of a stable compact-type curve; the principal component in the present star is R.
    We compute the specialization directly. For a smooth q0 ∈ Ei , lift it étale-locally to a section q.
The divisor Ei is Cartier on the regular total surface and

            OC (Ei )|Ei = OEi (−ei ),      OC (Ei )|R = OR (ei ),          OC (Ei )|Ej = OEj   (j ̸= i).   (4.23)


                                                          30
Thus OC (q − σ + Ei ) has componentwise degree zero and agrees generically with the Abel point.
Separatedness of J identifies its special value with α(q)0 . The restriction on Ei is OEi (q0 − ei ), all
other elliptic restrictions are trivial, and OR (ei − σ0 ) is trivial. There is no gluing torus in (4.18). A
section through R maps to zero, and continuity includes the nodes. Identifying Pic0 (Ei ) with Ei
using ei , we get
            α0 (R) = 0,       α0 (q ∈ Ei ) = (0, . . . , OEi (q − ei ), . . . , 0),   α0 (|C0 |) = |Z0 |.   (4.24)
Here Z0 is exactly the three-axis star of Lemma 4.3.
    Let A ⊂ J be the scheme-theoretic closure of the generic Abel image. Its ideal is the contraction
of the generic ideal. At a stalk over any base DVR its quotient embeds in its localization obtained
by inverting a uniformizer, and is therefore torsion-free over that DVR, hence flat. The ideal is
coherent by Noetherianity. The morphism α is proper, and Cη is dense in C. Its closed topological
image is therefore the closure of its generic image, so (4.24) gives
                                                   |A0 | = |Z0 |.                                           (4.25)
No reducedness of A0 is claimed. On the punctured base the relative Abel map is a closed immersion.
Indeed, it is proper with finite fibers, since every smooth-fiber Abel map is an embedding, so it
is finite by the cited finite-fiber criterion. Finite pushforward commutes with base change by its
affine description, and the fiber maps OJs → αs,∗ OCs are surjective. Nakayama’s lemma makes the
total map surjective. Its image is flat there and thus has a saturated ideal, so it agrees with the
restriction of the generic closure. Hence the noncentral As are the actual Abel curves schemewise.
     Put Hn = (Z/n)2 . Choose isomorphisms Hn ≃ Ei [n] and embed Hn diagonally in J0 [n] =
Q
   i Ei [n]. Denote its two cyclic direct factors by G1 , G2 . Multiplication by n on the abelian scheme
is proper, has invertible differential in characteristic zero and finite geometric fibers, so its kernel
is finite étale. After an étale localization, the chosen torsion points extend to sections with their
group relations. They remain distinct, since equality of two sections of a finite étale scheme is open
and closed. Thus the two cyclic factors retain order n and trivial intersection. The entire central
Hn projects injectively into each Ei .
     For one cyclic factor, the translates tg A are flat over S, and their central supports are the
disjoint stars of Lemma 4.3. A pairwise intersection is closed in the proper scheme J /S, so its
image in S is closed and misses 0. Removing finitely many such images makes all these translates
disjoint. Their disjoint union Z is then a closed flat subscheme of J , with actual Abel orbits on
noncentral fibers and
                                                          [

                                                           g
Apply the same argument to the negative closure −A for the other factor; its central support is still
Z0 . This reasoning uses the closure and its support, and does not assume that scheme-theoretic
image or union formation commutes with the closed-fiber base change.
    Vanishing for every Picard twist. We now have flat orbit closures and a line D representing
twice the principal polarization on every fiber. It remains to make the central vanishing uniform on

    Only the two within-part disjointness assertions are required; curves from the two different parts
may meet. They never coincide. In fact, if ts Cp = tt Σp , then the fixed degree-two line ωC (−2p−s+t)
has an effective divisor containing every varying q ∈ C. It must have at least two independent
sections, contrary to nonhyperellipticity. Hence every Ci and every t−x Σj have zero-dimensional
intersection or are disjoint, for every x ∈ X.

4.3      The relative Fourier–Mukai kernel and its coherent dual
Fix a seed supplied by Proposition 4.1. We now realize the cohomological argument following
that proposition: first identify the fixed kernel as a flat family of product ideals, then use its fiber
amplitude to construct the coherent dual.

4.3.1     The fixed transform and its kernel
Put M = X × b    X. A point (x, N ) of this product will parameterize a translation and a Picard
twist in one fixed kernel on the chosen Jacobian X. The flatness proved below concerns this family
over M ; deformation of the sixfold itself is treated later. We use the derived category of bounded
coherent complexes and the following fixed equivalence:

                           Ψ(F ) = R pr b
                                       X,∗

At first the last tensor product is derived. Expanding the shear in (4.29) changes IΣ H ⊠ IA H into

This is the fixed-kernel form of [25, (6.2.3) and (9.2.1)].

Lemma 4.5 (Local Tor and product ideals). Let V be a smooth threefold and let D1 , D2 ⊂ V be
smooth curves with finite, possibly empty, intersection. Then

                      TorO V
                         k (ID1 , ID2 ) = 0     (k > 0),      ID1 ⊗ ID2 ,−→ OV                 (4.33)

by multiplication, with image ID1 ID2 . The quotient from OV /ID1 ID2 to the structure sheaf of the
reduced union has zero-dimensional kernel. The same statements hold when each Di is a disjoint
union of smooth curves.

Proof. At an intersection point choose a regular sequence f1 , f2 for D1 . Its Koszul resolution
restricted to the regular one-dimensional local ring of D2 has injective left arrow: at least one
f¯i is nonzero because the intersection is zero-dimensional, and that local ring is a domain. Thus
Tork (OD1 , OD2 ) = 0 for k ≥ 2. The two ideal sequences give

                          Tork (ID1 , ID2 ) = Tork+2 (OD1 , OD2 ) = 0   (k > 0)

and Tor1 (OD1 , ID2 ) = 0. Tensoring the first ideal sequence with ID2 proves the injection and
product image in (4.33). Away from the intersection the product ideal equals the reduced-union
ideal, proving the assertion about its kernel. For disjoint unions, at any point at most one component
from each part is present, so the same local calculation applies. This is the local fact behind [25,
Appendix 11, pp. 91–93], in the form needed here.

Proposition 4.6 (The relative kernel and all its base changes). For the seed of Proposition 4.1,
the derived tensor in (4.31) is the ordinary coherent sheaf I J ⊂ OX×M . Both this ideal and its
quotient in OX×M are flat over M . Ideal multiplication and both ideal sequences commute with
every base change T → M . In particular, F is M -flat, and at m = (x, N ) it has the ordinary fiber

               Fm = IZx ⊗ Mx,N ,
                                                           Mx,N = H ⊗2 ⊗ a(−x) ⊗ N −1 .        (4.34)
                                          a
                IZx = IA IΣx ,     Σx =       t−x Σj ,
                                          j

Moreover G = Rπ∗ F has a local finite locally free model, and for every h : T → M ,

                                       Lh∗ G ≃ RπT,∗ (F |X×T ).                                (4.35)

Proof. The subscheme A × M is M -flat, and the subscheme defined by J is isomorphic to Σ × M
under (y, x, N ) 7→ (y + x, x, N ). Their ideal sequences make I and J M -flat. Locally along A × M ,
its regular codimension-two embedding gives the ideal resolution

                                   0 −→ O −→ O⊕2 −→ I −→ 0,                                    (4.36)

whose restriction to each fiber is the corresponding curve-ideal resolution. Tensor (4.36) with J .
On every fiber the left arrow is injective by Lemma 4.5.


                                                     33
    We use the following precise local flatness criterion. For a local map R → S ′ with S ′ Noetherian,
a finite S ′ -module Q, an R-flat module P , and an R-linear map u : Q → P , injectivity modulo
the maximal ideal of R implies injectivity of u and R-flatness of its cokernel [39, Lemma 10.99.1,
Tag 00MD]. Its mechanism is useful here: flatness compares the maximal-ideal filtrations and gives
injectivity modulo every power; Krull intersection gives injectivity. Repeating modulo every ideal
of R and using the Tor criterion gives flatness of the cokernel. Apply it at a total-space stalk to
J → J ⊕2 , with R = OM,m . It proves that the tensor of (4.36) is exact on the left and has M -flat
cokernel. Where I = O the conclusion is immediate. Thus the derived tensor is the ordinary
M -flat tensor.
    Its multiplication map to OX×M is injective on every fiber by Lemma 4.5. The same criterion,
now with the M -flat target OX×M , gives an injection with M -flat quotient. Tensoring either exact
sequence with any OT stays exact because its quotient is M -flat. This proves multiplication and
arbitrary base change before pushforward. The fiber formula follows from t∗x H = H ⊗ a(−x) and
P|X×{N } = N . Finally M is smooth and regular and π is projective. Lemma 4.4, applied to the
actual flat F , gives the local finite model and (4.35).

    The scheme Zx may have embedded points at intersections. Lemma 4.5 says that its reduction
is A ∪ Σx and that the difference has zero-dimensional support. In particular Zx always has
one-dimensional support.

4.3.2   The shifted dual is an ordinary sheaf
Proposition 4.7 (The actual coherent dual sheaf). Let DM (−) = RHomM (−, OM ). Every derived

is an actual coherent perfect sheaf on M , locally with a length-one locally free resolution, of rank

4.4    The coherent translation action and the quotient
For the group action, enumerate the source cosets in (4.9) by G2 and G1 , respectively. Translation
on a closed invariant subscheme gives its ideal the canonical translation linearization, so I = IΣ ⊠ IA
has a genuine G1 × G2 -linearization for the source translations. The word “coherent” in the next
statement includes the composition isomorphisms, not only one isomorphism for each group element.
Proposition 4.8 (Coherent conjugation and the graph quotient). For g = (g1 , g2 ) ∈ G1 × G2 , put

                           bg = (g1 − g2 , a(−g1 − g2 )) ∈ M,
                                                                                                 (4.46)
                          Ng = pr∗X a(−g1 ) ⊗ pr∗Xb P−g2 ,       Pv = P|{v}×Xb .

The conjugation of the source translations by Φ
                                              e has explicit functors

                                         Tg (−) = Ng ⊗ tbg ,∗ (−).                               (4.47)

There are unital associative line isomorphisms

                                          Ng ⊗ tbg ,∗ Nh ≃ Ng+h                                  (4.48)

and compatible isomorphisms Tg G ≃ G. Perfect duality gives actual sheaf isomorphisms

                                           Ng−1 ⊗ tbg ,∗ E ≃ E                                   (4.49)

with the corresponding unital associative line compositors. The homomorphism g 7→ bg is injective.
Its image Ḡ has order n2 , acts freely by translations, and

                                          q : M −→ Y0 = M/Ḡ                                     (4.50)

is a finite étale isogeny of abelian sixfolds.

                                                    35
Proof. We verify the conjugation at kernels, which also accounts for its compositors. For ℓ ∈ X
                                                                                              b
write Lℓ = P|X×{ℓ} . The normalized biextension has the kernel identities

                                    (tv × 1)∗ P −1 ≃ P −1 ⊗ pr∗Xb P−v ,
                                                                                                    (4.51)
                                   (1 × t−ℓ )∗ P −1 ≃ P −1 ⊗ pr∗X Lℓ .

Projection formula and flat base change for translation, applied to these kernels, give

                        Ψ ◦ tv,∗ ≃ (P−v ⊗ −) ◦ Ψ,         Ψ ◦ (Lℓ ⊗ −) ≃ tℓ,∗ ◦ Ψ.                  (4.52)

These are the inverse-Poincaré versions of the translation–tensor exchange in [29, Section 3, (3.1)].
Tensor conjugation on the source is

                           (H ⊗ −) ◦ tv,∗ ◦ (H −1 ⊗ −) ≃ (a(−v) ⊗ −) ◦ tv,∗ .                       (4.53)

For the shear,

                  µ∗ (a(u) ⊠ a(v)) ≃ a(u) ⊠ a(u + v),         µ−1 t(g1 ,g2 ) µ = t(g1 −g2 ,g2 ) .   (4.54)

Thus tensor conjugation and shear give the line a(−g1 ) ⊠ a(−g1 − g2 ) and the translation (g1 − g2 , g2 ).
Applying (4.52) on the second factor gives (4.46)–(4.47). Commuting the target translation past
P−g2 introduces only a constant one-dimensional biextension fiber; choosing its trivialization changes
the graph kernel identification by a scalar.
    All the displayed identities are identities of integral kernels: they are obtained by pullback,
projection formula, and the normalized biextension multiplication. That multiplication is associative.
Convolution with the fixed kernel of Φ   e and its inverse therefore transports the genuine source-
translation compositors and their associativity diagrams. The kernel of each functor in (4.47) is
an invertible sheaf on the graph of tbg . The product kernel and the kernel for g + h have the same
graph, since bg+h = bg + bh . A degree-zero derived isomorphism between these line sheaves is an
actual line isomorphism. Any two choices differ by a scalar, since H 0 (M, OM )× = C× . Transporting
the compositors through the chosen graph identifications consequently gives (4.48) with its unital
associativity, and transports the source linearization to Tg G ≃ G. This does not infer a scalar
composition from simplicity of G or E; the scalars come from the line kernels and their compositors.
    Duality commutes with translation by an isomorphism and changes a line factor to its inverse.
Dualize the preceding compatible isomorphisms and invert them. With the shift in (4.37) this
gives (4.49) and the inherited associative line compositors. Because (4.37) has only its degree-zero
cohomology sheaf, these are actual sheaf isomorphisms.
    Finally, bg = 0 implies g1 = g2 and 2g1 = 0, since a is injective. The intersection G1 ∩ G2 = 0
therefore makes the kernel trivial. Nonzero translations have no fixed points. The quotient by
this finite flat closed subgroup exists [13, Section 2, p. 136]; its quotient map is a Ḡ-torsor, hence
finite étale because that holds after the trivializing base change. The quotient is a proper smooth
connected group space. Over C it is a quasi-projective group scheme [13, Section 1.1(a), p. 131],
hence is a projective abelian variety. This proves (4.50).

    We have constructed the positive-rank coherent perfect sheaf E, its coherent line-twisted action
(4.49), and the finite étale quotient (4.50). The action is defined on the entire sheaf by the kernel
construction; neither simplicity nor reflexivity was needed. In the subsequent obstruction and
character calculations we write

                                    I = I,       G = G,       E = E.

                                                    36
The obstruction calculation applies to G, while the character and deformation arguments use its
coherent shifted dual E. The flat family constructed here belongs to the fixed seed. Subsequent
sections use semiregularity to deform an associated ordinary sheaf locally, and then propagate the
resulting algebraic classes through the period domain.


5     Hochschild evaluation and the invariant obstruction space
The geometric construction has produced a coherent perfect sheaf with a finite line-twisted action.
To use its quotient in a deformation argument, we must control the entire invariant derived
obstruction space. We first prove the exact Atiyah trace identity and its transport through the fixed
Fourier–Mukai equivalence, and then compute the invariant space for the Abel-orbit seed.

5.1     Hochschild evaluation and the Atiyah trace
We will relate the evaluation of a Hochschild class on a perfect complex to contraction against its
Chern character, and then compare these two maps under a fixed Fourier–Mukai equivalence. This is
the compatibility used in the obstruction argument of [25, Diagram (8.3.4) and Lemma 8.3.10]. The
proof below calculates the normalized Hochschild–Kostant–Rosenberg map on the formal diagonal
of an abelian variety. It then obtains the trace identity from the diagonal adjunction. In particular,
the calculation fixes one convention for the bivector sign on both sides.

5.1.1    Grading, contraction, and the first-jet class
Let A be a complex abelian variety of dimension d, let ∆ : A ,→ A × A be the diagonal, and let

in degree −1. Put

For a bundle it is the class of its first jet sequence. For a perfect complex it is the same derived
natural transformation. The power abQ : Q → Q ⊗ ΩbA [b] means successive applications of this class,
with each new one-form appended on the right, followed by the exterior projection. This fixes both
the Yoneda and exterior orders; a0Q is the identity.
   For a coefficient complex C, the perfect partial trace is

                                TrQ,C : Hom(Q, Q ⊗ C) −→ Hom(OA , C).

for f : Q → Q ⊗ C. These are the duality and symmetry maps of perfect complexes, with their usual
Koszul signs. On a bounded locally free representative this is supertrace. The definition applies
without change to shifted C, and it does not require a cohomology sheaf of Q to be locally free.

    The proof uses two identities on the diagonal. We first show that the normalized bar map carries
the Hochschild action to the fixed left contraction. We then show that the adjunction unit becomes
the exponential of the first-jet class. Naturality of the unit and of perfect trace will give the stated
triangle.

                                                               39
5.1.3   The normalized HKR action on the formal diagonal
We next verify globally the normalization and action of these maps on an abelian variety.
   The difference isomorphism

                              A × A −→ A × A,               (x, y) 7−→ (y, x − y)

takes ∆ to A×{0}, and hence identifies the formal completion along ∆ with A× Ab0 . In characteristic
zero a commutative formal group has logarithm coordinates. In this case they can be obtained
by integrating the closed invariant formal one-forms term by term, with constant term zero. The
differential of the difference between the logarithm of the formal sum and the sum of the two
logarithms is zero, and its constant term is zero; thus the logarithm carries the formal group law to
addition. Its linear term is invertible. Choose coordinates z1 , . . . , zd whose linear terms are a basis
of invariant one-forms. The completed structure sheaf is then

                                       R = OA ⊗
                                              b C C[[z1 , . . . , zd ]].

These are formal normal coordinates. No global nonconstant regular or holomorphic functions on
A × A cutting out ∆ are asserted.
   We explain why this completion computes the derived operations that will be used. Noetherian
completion is flat. If R is a Noetherian local ring along the diagonal, J is its diagonal ideal, and
F → R/J is a finite free resolution, then Rb ⊗R F remains a resolution and

                                      b ⊗R F, R/J) = HomR (F, R/J).
                                HomRb(R                                                              (5.11)

The same equality holds with a locally free R/J-module as target. Such finite resolutions exist
locally because the diagonal of the smooth variety A is a regular immersion. The identifications in
(5.11) commute with composition and with tensoring by R/J. They therefore identify the derived
sheaf Hom complexes on the diagonal, their Yoneda pairings, and the action on L∆∗ O∆ . After sheaf
cohomology they identify the corresponding global operations. In particular, the diagonal-supported
morphisms with locally free diagonal coefficients below can be compared after completion and then
descended by these identifications.
    Let ei have cohomological degree −1 and correspond to dzi , and let ξi be the dual invariant
vector. A global finite free resolution on the formal completion is
                                                      !
                             ^•
                                   ∗
                                             X
                K=      R⊗        V , dK =        zi Ci ,        Ci = ιξi ,       ε : K −→ OA .      (5.12)

                   V

                                                                ^•

    It remains to identify this splitting with the normalized bar map, rather than merely with some

Taking the last point as base, use the formal logarithm to write z (0) , . . . , z (n) for the logarithms of
the other points minus the last point. The outer R-action uses z (0) . Each completed bar term is a
formal power series extension of the Noetherian sheaf R, hence is flat over R. The ordinary bar

                                                      40
contracting homotopy after forgetting one outer action inserts a unit. It is continuous for these
formal-adic topologies, so its identities extend to the completion. Thus the augmented completed
bar complex is a flat resolution of the diagonal.
   There is a global map from K to this resolution whose term in degree −n is

An interior bar face identifies two successive middle points, so the alternating determinant on the
right becomes zero. The last face sets z (n) = 0, again giving zero. The first face sets z (1) = z (0) .
Expansion along that column gives, for I = (i1 , . . . , in ),
                                       n
                                       X              (0)
                         bFn (eI ) =         (−1)ℓ−1 ziℓ Fn−1 (eI\iℓ ) = Fn−1 (dK eI ).
                                       ℓ=1

In degree zero the map is the identity on the augmentation. It is therefore a comparison of resolutions.
It is global on the formal neighborhood: the logarithm makes every face substitution just used
compatible on that neighborhood.
    The normalized local bar formula defining IA restricts to this completed resolution. Indeed, it
uses only the first jet in each middle variable along the small diagonal, so is continuous for the
completion; naturality of differentials identifies the formulas on overlaps. After pullback to the
diagonal, each term of (5.14) maps to dzi1 ∧ · · · ∧ dzin /n!: the permutation sign and the exterior
sign cancel. The n! terms sum to dzI . Consequently IA is the identity eI 7→ dzI on the first complex
in (5.13). In particular it is a quasi-isomorphism. Because the comparison (5.14) is global, this
conclusion has no additional off-diagonal or higher-cohomology component. This is the restriction
of the usual sheafified bar HKR morphism, not a newly chosen splitting.
    We now compute its cap action. The Ci have degree 1, satisfy

                                Ci Cj = −Cj Ci ,            dK Ci + Ci dK = 0,

and, for u = ξ1 ∧ · · · ∧ ξb , give a closed lift CuL = Cξ1 · · · Cξb . Its augmentation on K −b is sb times
determinant evaluation. More explicitly, the map
                                       ^•
                               OA ⊗           V −→ EndR (K),                u 7−→ CuL

is a morphism of differential graded algebras and is a quasi-isomorphism. To see the last assertion,
postcompose with ε. The resulting map to HomR (K, OA ) is the basis isomorphism given by the
graded dual pairing. The map EndR (K) → HomR (K, OA ) is itself a quasi-isomorphism, because
K is bounded free and ε is a quasi-isomorphism. This gives global closed lifts for the sheaf-level
Yoneda and cap pairings.
    For clarity about global cohomology, first perform the derived Hom and derived pullback
with the finite free complex K. The two resulting complexes in (5.13) are finite complexes of
trivial OA -bundles. The closed lifts CuL compute their sheaf-level pairings. Resolve only these
OA -coefficients by the ordinary Dolbeault resolution A0,•
                                                        A , and totalize the pairings with Dolbeault
wedge. The Dolbeault Poincaré lemma computes their sheaf cohomology. Projective GAGA for
coherent cohomology and Ext identifies these cohomology groups and pairings with their algebraic
counterparts. Neither a Dolbeault resolution of the infinite formal sheaf nor GAGA for such a sheaf
is used.
    Every class in H a (A, OA ) has an invariant Dolbeault representative; these representatives form
Va 1
    H (A, OA ). This standard computation for a complex torus, together with the triviality of TA ,

                                                         41
shows that decomposable classes η ⊗ u span HT r (A). For such a class the totalized operator on a
normal Koszul term k and a Dolbeault cochain ω is
                              Lη,u (ω ⊗ k) = (−1)b|ω| (η ∧ ω) ⊗ CuL (k).                       (5.15)
This formula denotes the induced pairing on the finite diagonal complexes above. The normal lift is
closed by its anticommutation with dK ; totalization is closed because η is closed. Its augmentation
is the cochain for the graded dual pairing. The sign moves the degree-b normal operator past the
Dolbeault cochain.
    Take the Yoneda convention β ⌣ γ = β[|γ|] ◦ γ. Composing (5.15) gives the first product in
(5.4), because CuL CvL = Cu∧v
                          L . Tensoring the same lift with the diagonal gives precisely (5.3). The

roof for η∆ on K has denominator ε : K → OA and numerator the projection K → OA ⊗R K.
The augmentation just calculated is therefore the adjoint class (5.6). Derived adjunction and the
quasi-isomorphism IA show that HKRA : HT r (A) → HH r (A) is an isomorphism. The calculation
proves, in particular,
               IA (L∆∗ HKRA (α))h = α ⌟ IA (h),            α ∈ HT 2 (A),   h ∈ H0 (A, HA ).

Here a derived endomorphism acts on hypercohomology by composition. The same calculation of

                                         sb X

Indeed, evaluation on Fb gives sb times determinant evaluation, the augmentation of CuL . This
is the cochain convention in [6, Equation (3.20)]. In particular s2 = −1; replacing left interior

by diag(1, 1, −1) on H 2 (OA ) ⊕ H 1 (TA ) ⊕ H 0 ( 2 TA ). We make no such replacement.
                                                  V

    The formal coordinates, their comparison, and (5.15) are fixed by diagonal translation. Transla-
tions also fix invariant Dolbeault representatives. Thus translations act trivially on HT r (A), and
HKRA identifies this with their action on HH r (A). Finally, TApis holomorphically trivial, so its
positive Chern classes and its Atiyah class vanish and td(A) = td(A) = 1. The explicit identity
(5.16) is the abelian specialization of the compatibility in [6, Theorem 1.4]; it was proved here
without using the general formality theorem or a separate convention for dual chains.

For a vector bundle, the first infinitesimal diagonal is finite over the second factor, so its evaluation

If αa,b ∈ H a (A, b TA ), its morphism from SA is zero on all summands except ΩbA [b]. Its value in

(5.21) is exactly αa,b ⌟ (abQ /b!), of Ext degree a + b, with the graded dual and total-complex signs

    The components in (5.7) are the unscaled d log Hodge components. To identify them with the
ordinary character, first consider a line bundle. Its first jet extension is the d log transition cocycle;
thus (5.7) is the exponential of that first Hodge Chern class. Perfect trace is additive for a bounded
locally free exact triangle, as is seen from supertrace on its cone. Pullback naturality of the first-jet
class and the projective bundle formula allow a vector bundle to be pulled to its flag bundle, where
its successive line quotients give the line-bundle calculation. Pullback on the Hodge cohomology in
question is injective by that formula. Finally, the resolution property of a smooth projective variety
represents every perfect class by an alternating sum of vector bundles; additivity treats Q. This
gives the character identification of [7, Theorem 4.5] in the present convention.
    Let chB,j (Q) ∈ H 2j (A, Q) denote the rational Betti Chern component. Under the Dolbeault–de
Rham identification H j (A, ΩjA ) = H j,j (A) ⊂ H 2j (A, C), the raw comparison is

                                     chH,j (Q) 7−→ (2πi)j chB,j (Q).                                 (5.22)

Indeed, the exponential sequence with kernel 2πiZ sends the d log cocycle to 2πi cB
                                                                                  1 , and the line
splitting and additivity just used give the formula in every weight. Dividing by (2πi)j is the
Tate-normalized comparison. The trace identity (5.9) uses chH throughout.

5.2     Fourier–Mukai transport of evaluation and contraction
We now show that a specified Fourier–Mukai equivalence preserves the equality ker obQ = ker cQ .
Evaluation is transported by convolution of kernels. For character contraction we construct a
homological map using perfect Serre pairings; this also records the sign of the shift in the fixed
transform.

                                                   44
5.2.1   Enhanced convolution and evaluation
Let A and B be smooth projective complex abelian varieties of dimensions dA , dB . Let Φ = ΦK :
Perf(A) → Perf(B) be a Fourier–Mukai equivalence with perfect kernel K, equipped with a fixed
enhanced inverse kernel L, inverse adjunction units and counits, and their triangle identities. Here
enhanced means that convolution, morphisms, and the isomorphisms commuting with cohomological
shifts are those of the derived enhancement, not just chosen identifications of objects in the
triangulated category.
    For kernels P from X to Y and R from Y to Z, write

                               R ⋆ P = RpXZ∗ Lp∗XY P ⊗L Lp∗Y Z R .
                                                                        


Thus ΦR⋆P = ΦR ◦ ΦP . We use the standard enhanced associativity and projection formula for this
convolution. The products are smooth proper and the projections are proper perfect morphisms, so
these operations send perfect kernels to perfect kernels. The fixed adjunctions give

                                 K ⋆ L ≃ O ∆B ,        L ⋆ K ≃ O ∆A .

Conjugation
                       CΦ (M ) = K ⋆ M ⋆ L : Perf(A × A) −→ Perf(B × B)
                                                                                            ∼
is an exact enhanced equivalence, with the opposite conjugation as inverse. Let t : CΦ (O∆A ) −
                                                                                              → O ∆B
be its counit identification. For β ∈ HH r (A), define

                             ΦHH (β) = t[r] ◦ CΦ (β) ◦ t−1 ∈ HH r (B).                          (5.25)

It is a graded isomorphism and preserves our Yoneda convention, because every displayed shifted
morphism uses the enhanced shift isomorphism.
                                              ∼
    For Q ∈ Perf(A), let ΦExt : Ext2A (Q, Q) −→ Ext2B (ΦQ, ΦQ) be the induced map with the same
shift structure. Associativity of convolution, evaluated on Q, gives

                          χΦQ (ΦHH β) = ΦExt (χQ (β)),        β ∈ HH 2 (A).                     (5.26)

Indeed, evaluation on ΦQ is the composite K ⋆ β ⋆ L ⋆ K evaluated on Q. The inverse unit and counit
cancel the adjacent L ⋆ K by the adjunction triangle identities. The outer t then gives exactly the
morphism obtained by applying Φ to the evaluation on Q. This proves the equality on morphisms,
including their shifts, rather than only identifying the dimensions of Ext groups.
    Use the fixed HKR maps of Proposition 5.1 to put
                                                                   ∼
                      ΦHT = (HKRB )−1 ΦHH HKRA : HT 2 (A) −
                                                          → HT 2 (B).

The evaluation square is already established. The contraction map has the corresponding compati-
bility, with the homological comparison map constructed below.

                                     ΦHT (ker obQ ) = ker obΦQ ,

In particular ker obQ = ker cQ implies ker obΦQ = ker cΦQ .

will also retain the Chern-character sign of a shift in the equivalence.

complex.

Smoothness, properness, and perfection are exactly the hypotheses for these duality and adjunction
statements.

isomorphisms in (5.26).

independently normalized Betti integral transform.

                                      A=            Cj ,        Σ=           Σj
                                              j=1                      j=1

of translates of an Abel curve and of its inverse, respectively. The cyclic groups G2 , G1 ⊂ X, each
of order n, act transitively on the components of A, Σ, respectively. Write
                               I = I Σ ⊠ IA    on X 2 ,              Gsrc = G1 × G2 .
Each ideal has its canonical translation linearization as the ideal of an invariant subscheme, and I
has the product linearization. On M = X × b   X, the fixed transform of Proposition 4.8 is
                                                            
                            e = Φ ◦ (H ⊠ H) ⊗ − ,
                            Φ                                          G = Φ(I)[−3].
                                                                           e

Write Ḡ ≃ Gsrc for the underlying translation group of its coherent line-translation action.
    We will prove that the semiregularity map of Proposition 5.1 is injective on the full invariant
derived group Ext2M (G, G)Ḡ . The proof has two computations: the invariant group for I has
dimension 48, and contraction with its raw Hodge Chern character has rank 48. The exact
Hochschild–Atiyah identity then identifies the entire invariant group with the evaluation image. This
calculation concerns the Abel-orbit seed in Markman’s secant-sheaf construction [25, Sections 8.3–8.4];
we give the invariant calculation directly, using the independently movable orbit components.

                                                           49
5.3.1   The Abel class and its normal directions
An Abel curve in X will mean a translate of the image of an Abel embedding C ,→ X, or a translate
of its image under [−1], with the Jacobian and polarization conventions of Section 4.

Lemma 5.3. Let D ⊂ X be an Abel curve. Its normalized Betti class and Chern character are
                                         2
                                        θB                        2
                                                                 θB  θ3
                               [D]B =      ,     chB (OD ) =        − B.
                                         2                        2   3
Normal restriction of translation-invariant vector fields is an isomorphism
                                                 ∼
                                   H 0 (X, TX ) −−→ H 0 (D, ND/X );

both spaces have dimension three. If Z = nj=1 Dj is a disjoint union of n Abel curves, then its ideal
                                           `

satisfies the full normalized Betti identity
                                                          n 2   n 3
                                      chB (IZ ) = 1 −       θB + θB .                            (5.39)
                                                          2     3
Proof. The fundamental-class formula is (4.6), proved there from the Jacobian lattice. Translation
acts trivially on cohomology, and inversion acts by +1 on H 4 , so it holds for every Abel curve in
the stated sense.
   For the inclusion i : D ,→ X, use Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch in its smooth proper form
                                                                        
                                   ch(i∗ F ) td(X) = i∗ ch(F ) td(D)

for a closed immersion of smooth projective varieties; this is the form of the Riemann–Roch theorem
recorded by Borel and Serre [3]. The tangent bundle of X is trivial, and td(D) = 1 + 12 c1 (TD ). Since
deg TD = 2 − 2 · 3 = −4 and [pt]B = θB3 /6, it follows that

                                                                         2
                                                                        θB  θ3
                         chB (OD ) = i∗ td(D) = [D]B − 2[pt]B =            − B.
                                                                         2   3
For disjoint Z, one has OZ = j ODj . Additivity in 0 → IZ → OX → OZ → 0 gives (5.39).
                               L

   It remains to calculate the normal sections. For the original Abel embedding the normal sequence
is
                          0 −→ TC −→ OC ⊗ H 1 (C, OC ) −→ NC/X −→ 0.                          (5.40)
Here the middle bundle is TX |C . The dual of the first arrow is the evaluation map H 0 (C, KC ) ⊗
OC → KC : differentiating the Abel integral sends a tangent vector v to the functional ω 7→ ω(v).
Consequently, under Serre duality on C, the dual of the map

                              H 1 (C, TC ) −→ H 1 (C, OC ) ⊗ H 1 (C, OC )

in the long exact sequence of (5.40) is the multiplication map

                             H 0 (C, KC ) ⊗ H 0 (C, KC ) −→ H 0 (C, KC2 ).

This is the usual cohomological Serre duality [36], applied to the dual evaluation map.
    For completeness, multiplication is onto here by an elementary genus-three argument. For an
effective divisor D2 of length two, curve Riemann–Roch, in the form h0 (L) − h0 (KC ⊗ L−1 ) =
deg L + 1 − g, gives
                                   h0 (KC − D2 ) = h0 (D2 ) = 1.

                                                     50
The last equality uses nonhyperellipticity. Two independent sections of a degree-two effective line,
after removing their common base divisor, would give a map to P1 of degree at most two. Degree
one would make C rational, and degree two would make it hyperelliptic. Thus KC separates
every length-two subscheme. It has three sections and degree four, so it embeds C as an integral
plane quartic. Such a curve is not contained in a conic. Restriction of quadrics therefore makes
Sym2 H 0 (KC ) → H 0 (KC2 ) injective. Both spaces have dimension six, the second by curve Riemann–
Roch and deg KC2 = 8 > 2g − 2. The multiplication map is consequently onto, and its dual is
injective.
    Since H 0 (C, TC ) = 0, the long exact sequence of (5.40) now identifies H 0 (C, NC/X ) with the
middle bundle’s constant sections H 1 (C, OC ) = H 0 (X, TX ). This is exactly normal restriction of
translation-invariant vector fields. Translation and inversion carry the normal sequence and this
isomorphism to every Abel curve.

5.3.2     The full invariant derived Ext group
We next compute the derived deformation space of one orbit ideal. The calculation keeps the
local-to-global contribution of the normal bundle; it does not replace derived Ext by the cohomology
of an endomorphism sheaf.

Lemma 5.4. Let Γ ⊂ X be a finite subgroup of order n, and let Z = nj=1 Dj be a disjoint union of
                                                                                   `

Abel curves permuted transitively by Γ. Give IZ its canonical linearization. There is an isomorphism
of Γ-representations
                                                                     n
                                                                     M
                              Ext1X (IZ , IZ ) ≃ H 1 (X, OX ) ⊕            H 0 (X, TX ).                    (5.41)
                                                                     j=1

The first summand is trivial, and Γ acts on the second by its regular permutation action on the
components and the identity on each translation space. In particular,

           dimC Ext1X (IZ , IZ )Γ = 6,     dimC Ext2X (IZ , IZ )Γ = 6,              HomX (IZ , IZ )Γ = C.

Proof. At a point of Z, write its regular codimension-two ideal in the regular local ring R = OX,x
as I = (f, g), where f, g are part of a regular system of parameters. It has the resolution
                                              (−g,f )        (f,g)
                                    0 −→ R −−−−→ R2 −−−→ I −→ 0.

If an endomorphism sends f, g to u, v, its relation is gu = f v. Regularity of f, g gives u = f r,
v = gr for a unique r ∈ R, so HomR (I, I) = R. Applying HomR (−, I) to the resolution shows that
the degree-one cokernel is I/I 2 . Under a change of generators, the dual of the leftmost generator
acquires the inverse determinant. Consequently the global degree-one sheaf is

                             (IZ /IZ2 ) ⊗ det(IZ /IZ2 )−1 ≃ (IZ /IZ2 )∨ = NZ/X .

The rank-two isomorphism sends v ⊗ ℓ∨ to the functional w 7→ ℓ∨ (v ∧ w), so it is compatible with
changes of generators. We view NZ/X here as a sheaf on X supported on Z. Away from Z the ideal
is OX , and the resolution has length one. Thus, globally,

        HomX (IZ , IZ ) = OX ,      Ext1X (IZ , IZ ) = NZ/X ,          ExtqX (IZ , IZ ) = 0   (q ≥ 2).      (5.42)

   The standard local-to-global spectral sequence

                            E2p,q = H p (X, ExtqX (IZ , IZ )) =⇒ Extp+q
                                                                    X (IZ , IZ )


                                                        51
is the hypercohomology spectral sequence of RHomX (IZ , IZ ). Its five-term sequence and (5.42) give
                                                            e
               0 −→ H 1 (X, OX ) −→ Ext1X (IZ , IZ ) −
                                                     → H 0 (Z, NZ/X ) −→ H 2 (X, OX ).              (5.43)

The first arrow is also the map induced by the perfect unit OX → RHom(IZ , IZ ): the perfect trace
composed with this unit is multiplication by the rank, which is one.
    Translate the n curves independently. The locus in X n where these translated proper curves
remain disjoint is an open neighborhood of the original tuple. Over it their union is a disjoint union
of flat translated families. Its ideal is flat as well, by the exact sequence from the structure sheaf of
the union. Restriction of this family to first-order tangent vectors gives a linear map
                                      n
                                      M
                                 s:         H 0 (X, TX ) −→ Ext1X (IZ , IZ ).
                                      j=1

Indeed a flat deformation over C[ϵ]/(ϵ2 ) gives the extension 0 → IZ → IZ,ϵ → IZ → 0. For a vector
field ξ, the moving generators are f − ϵξ(f ) and g − ϵξ(g). Lifting the displayed relation (−g, f )
gives                                                                   
                        (−g) f − ϵξ(f ) + f g − ϵξ(g) = ϵ gξ(f ) − f ξ(g) .
Thus the local Ext cocycle is represented by gξ(f ) − f ξ(g) ∈ I/I 2 , up to the single sign in the
chosen Ext boundary convention. Choose ℓ = f¯ ∧ ḡ and ℓ∨ (ℓ) = 1. Under the written determinant
identification v ⊗ ℓ∨ 7→ (w 7→ ℓ∨ (v ∧ w)), this displayed representative sends f¯, ḡ to −ξ(f ), −ξ(g),
respectively. Reversing the boundary convention reverses both signs. Consequently the edge map
e sends the geometric deformation to its normal restriction up to one fixed sign, independent
of the vector field and the component. By Lemma 5.3, e ◦ s is therefore the direct sum of the
normal-restriction isomorphisms multiplied by that fixed sign. It follows that (5.43) reduces to the
split short exact sequence
                                                                e
                     0 −→ H 1 (X, OX ) −→ Ext1X (IZ , IZ ) −
                                                           → H 0 (Z, NZ/X ) −→ 0,

with splitting given by s after this signed normal-restriction identification.
    This splitting is equivariant. Translation acts trivially on H 1 (X, OX ), as is seen from translation-
invariant Dolbeault representatives, and it acts as the identity on H 0 (X, TX ). It sends the deforma-
tion of Dj to the deformation of its translated component. Since a group of order n acts transitively
on n components, that permutation action is regular. This proves (5.41); its invariant summands
have dimensions three and three.
    We also use Serre duality for perfect complexes on a smooth projective threefold:

                                  Exti (F, G)∨ ≃ Ext3−i (G, F ⊗ ωX ).

This is the extension of the usual locally free Serre duality [36] by finite locally free resolutions.
Choose a nonzero translation-invariant holomorphic three-form on X. For F = G = IZ , the resulting
perfect pairing is
                         Ext1 (IZ , IZ ) ⊗ Ext2 (IZ , IZ ) −→ H 3 (X, OX ) ≃ C.
It is Γ-invariant: translations preserve the chosen form, composition, and the perfect trace. Thus
these two Ext groups are dual Γ-representations. Averaging over the finite group in characteristic
zero identifies invariants of the dual with the dual of the invariants, so the invariant Ext2 also has
dimension six. Finally (5.42) gives Hom(IZ , IZ ) = H 0 (X, OX ) = C, with trivial action.



                                                       52
Proposition 5.5. For the source object I = IΣ ⊠ IA and its canonical Gsrc = G1 × G2 linearization,

                              dimC Ext2X 2 (I, I)Gsrc = 6 + 36 + 6 = 48.

This is the invariant subspace of the full derived Ext group.

Proof. The ideal sheaves are perfect by their local length-one resolutions. The perfect Künneth
isomorphism for their derived global Hom complexes is

                     R HomX 2 (I, I) ≃ R HomX (IΣ , IΣ ) ⊗L
                                                          C R HomX (IA , IA ).

Taking degree two gives

                          Ext2X 2 (I, I) ≃ Ext2X (IΣ , IΣ ) ⊗ HomX (IA , IA )
                                           ⊕ Ext1X (IΣ , IΣ ) ⊗ Ext1X (IA , IA )
                                           ⊕ HomX (IΣ , IΣ ) ⊗ Ext2X (IA , IA ).                         (5.44)

Here the isomorphism follows by taking cohomology of the tensor product over C of the two derived
global Hom complexes; over the field C there are no additional Tor terms. It is equivariant for
the independent actions of G1 , G2 . Averaging in each factor gives (V1 ⊗ V2 )G1 ×G2 = V1G1 ⊗ V2G2 .
Applying Lemma 5.4 to the three summands of (5.44) gives 6, 6 · 6, and 6. Because (5.44) is a
decomposition of derived Ext itself, the count retains every local-to-global contribution.

5.3.3   The rank of contraction with the raw Hodge character
We now compute the map that Proposition 5.1 factors through this 48-dimensional invariant group.
Use the gradings (5.1); in particular, for an abelian variety B,

                                                   H q (B, ΩpB )
                                             M

                                           q−p=k

For a perfect object Q on B, extend character contraction to every degree k ≥ 0 by

                        ckQ : HT k (B) −→ HΩ−k (B),         ckQ (α) = α ⌟ chH (Q).

Here chH is the raw first-jet character (5.7), and contraction uses the left-interior action (5.3),
including its Dolbeault Koszul sign. Thus c2Q = cQ in Proposition 5.1.
    The normalization of the character matters for that identity. Put θH = aH ∈ H 1 (X, Ω1X ). Under
the raw de Rham comparison,

               θH 7−→ (2πi)θB ,      chH,k (Q) 7−→ (2πi)k chB,k (Q)      in codimension k.               (5.45)

There is no codimension-one component. Each term in (5.46) has the raw weight specified by (5.45).
   Use the abelian Hodge-cohomology identifications

These follow from the invariant Dolbeault forms on the complex torus. The spaces U, W, T each have

                                   j : T −→ U,      j(ξ) = ξ ⌟ θH
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 5.6. For either orbit ideal IZ , the map c1IZ is injective of rank six, and c2IZ is surjective of
rank six.

Proof. An element of HT 1 (X) = U ⊕ T is a pair (η, ξ). Using (5.46), its projections under c1IZ to
H 1 (OX ) and H 2 (Ω1X ) are respectively

                                         η,      −n j(ξ) ∧ θH .                                   (5.47)

The first uses the rank term of the character; the second uses −nθH    2 /2. There is no contribution

from ξ to the first projection or from η to the second, because the codimension-one character is
zero. The signs in (5.47) are those of the specified left-interior convention.
   The map v 7→ v ∧ θH on U is injective. If v ̸= 0, choose bases with v = u1 and θH = 3i=1 ui ⊗ wi ,
                                                                                         P

absorbing the fixed contraction signs in the dual basis. The terms of v ∧ θH with i = 2, 3 are linearly
independent nonzero tensors. Therefore the two projections in (5.47) determine η and ξ, proving
the first assertion.
   The only summands of HΩ−2 (X) are

                          H 2 (X, OX ) = ∧2 U,      H 3 (X, Ω1X ) = ∧3 U ⊗ W.                     (5.48)

Each has dimension three. The H 2 (OX ) = ∧2 U block of HT 2 (X) maps identically to the first
summand, using the rank term fn,0 = 1. The H 1 (TX ) = U ⊗ T block has no contribution there,
since fn,1 = 0, and its projection to the second summand is

                                     η ⊗ ξ 7−→ −n η ∧ j(ξ) ∧ θH .                                 (5.49)

For each k ∈ {1, 2, 3}, take η and j(ξ) to be the two basis elements ui , uj complementary to uk .
Their wedge with θH is a nonzero multiple of (u1 ∧ u2 ∧ u3 ) ⊗ wk . Hence (5.49) spans the second
summand. These two source blocks already give rank six; the target in (5.48) has dimension six.
This proves surjectivity. The bivector block H 0 (∧2 TX ) is not needed for this lower bound.

Lemma 5.7. For I = IΣ ⊠ IA , the map

                                    c2I : HT 2 (X 2 ) −→ HΩ−2 (X 2 )

has rank 48.

Proof. Künneth gives the domain decomposition

       HT 2 (X 2 ) = HT 2 (X) ⊗ HT 0 (X) ⊕ HT 1 (X) ⊗ HT 1 (X) ⊕ HT 0 (X) ⊗ HT 2 (X) .
                                                                                            


The product character is fn ⊠ fn , and HT 0 (X) = C acts by c0IZ (1) = fn ̸= 0. The written
left-action and product formulas (5.3) and (5.4), applied to the Künneth decomposition, give the
external-product contraction rule with its total-complex signs. They therefore send the three blocks
onto
                    im(c2IΣ ) ⊗ Cfn ,    im(c1IΣ ) ⊗ im(c1IA ),  Cfn ⊗ im(c2IA ).

                                                   54
Their dimensions are 6, 36, 6, by Lemma 5.6. The target has the direct Künneth decomposition
                                               M
                             HΩ−2 (X 2 ) =            HΩ−r (X) ⊗ HΩ−s (X).
                                              r+s=2

The first image lies in the factorwise Hodge-imbalance summand (2, 0), the second in (1, 1), and
the third in (0, 2). Here the imbalance of H q (X, ΩpX ) is q − p; the two-factor Hodge Künneth
decomposition is a direct sum for these ordered pairs. Hence the three images cannot cancel,
regardless of their Künneth signs. Their ranks add to 6 + 36 + 6 = 48.

5.3.4   The exact rank comparison and the fixed transform
The two calculations now have the same dimension. We use the exact local Hochschild results to
turn this equality into injectivity on the whole invariant group. Write oQ = obQ for evaluation and
σQ for the perfect-trace map of Proposition 5.1. For a perfect object Q on an abelian variety B,
their domains and targets are
                                         oQ                      σQ
                             HT 2 (B) −−−→ Ext2B (Q, Q) −−−→ HΩ−2 (B).

Proposition 5.8. For the source I = IΣ ⊠ IA with its canonical Gsrc = G1 × G2 -linearization,

                          im(oI ) = Ext2X 2 (I, I)Gsrc ,        ker(oI ) = ker(c2I ),            (5.50)

and σI is injective on this full invariant group.
   Let M = X × X   b and use the fixed enhanced equivalence
                                                        
                            e = Φ ◦ (H ⊠ H) ⊗ − ,
                            Φ                                     G = Φ(I)[−3].
                                                                      e

Give G the coherent line-translation action from Proposition 4.8, and denote its underlying translation
group by Ḡ ≃ Gsrc . Then

                           im(oG ) = Ext2M (G, G)Ḡ ,          ker(oG ) = ker(c2G ),             (5.51)

and
                                      σG Ext2 (G,G)Ḡ       is injective.
                                              M

Proof. Proposition 5.1 proves for every perfect Q the exact identity

                                              σQ ◦ oQ = c2Q

with the definitions just given. In particular it is an identity for the perfect trace on derived Ext,
with the same contraction convention as the rank computation. The components with q > dim B − 2
vanish because H q+2 (B, ΩqB ) = 0.
    Translations act trivially on HT 2 (X 2 ): they fix the invariant tangent fields and the invariant
Dolbeault representatives of H a (OX 2 ). The translation-invariant diagonal-kernel HKR construction
identifies this with their trivial action on HH 2 (X 2 ). Apply the diagonal-kernel evaluation square
(5.26) to each translation equivalence, and identify its translate of I with I by the canonical
linearization. Naturality of evaluation then gives g · oI (α) = oI (g · α) = oI (α) for g ∈ Gsrc . Hence

                                  im(oI ) ⊂ Winv := Ext2X 2 (I, I)Gsrc .



                                                      55
Proposition 5.5 gives dim Winv = 48, while Lemma 5.7 and the exact identity give

                             48 = rank(c2I ) ≤ rank(oI ) ≤ dim Winv = 48.

Thus the inclusion is equality and both maps have rank 48. The identity also gives ker(oI ) ⊂ ker(c2I );
equality of ranks on their common domain makes the kernels equal. If w = oI (α) ∈ Winv and
σI (w) = 0, then c2I (α) = 0, so the kernel equality gives w = 0. This proves (5.50) and source

    Apply Proposition 5.2 to the fixed enhanced equivalence Q 7→ Φ(Q)[−3].
                                                                 e           It includes tensoring by

The map γ is the HKR expression of the enhanced Hochschild-homology isomorphism: its cap

identification of γ with a separately normalized Betti integral transform is used.

The first equality of (5.52) transports the source image equality to the image equality in (5.51).

    The fixed support construction identifies

                                       E = RHomM (G, OM )[−1]

as the actual coherent perfect sheaf of positive rank, with the dual coherent action on the same
underlying translation group. Proposition 7.2 uses the full injection just proved, transfers it by perfect
duality, and then performs finite étale twisted descent. The exact dual sign and that derived descent
are left to that proposition. The output here is injection on Ext2 (G, G)Ḡ itself, not only on a chosen
Hochschild subspace. No identification of that derived Ext group with H 2 (M, H0 (RHomM (G, G)))
is used.


6    The spin character and the polarized quadratic seed
The preceding construction gives a coherent perfect sheaf E of rank r = 8d on M = X × X.
                                                                                      b Its
