elements that do not preserve the initial Hodge structure.

6.1   The fixed transform and its source character
Put ℓX = 6 = 2gX , with gX = 3, and set

                      L = H 1 (X, Q),       U = L∨ = H 1 ( b
                                                          X, Q),                 V = L ⊕ U.
                           R
Choose e1 , . . . , eℓX with X e1 ∧ · · · ∧ eℓX = 1, and let f1 , . . . , fℓX be the dual basis of U . Our
normalized Poincaré line has class
                                                          ℓ
                                                          XX
                                        p = c1,B (P) =          ei ∧ f i .
                                                          i=1

For an ordered subset P ⊂ {1, . . . , ℓX }, write eP and fP for the corresponding increasing products.
For disjoint ordered subsets I, J, let ϵI,J be the sign in eI eJ = ϵI,J eI∪J , and set it to zero if they
overlap. Define reversal by
                                       τ (eP ) = (−1)|P |(|P |−1)/2 eP .
   We use Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch in the following standard form: if f : B → C is a proper
morphism of smooth projective complex varieties and F is perfect, then
                                                                                 
                                chB (Rf∗ F ) td(C) = f∗ chB (F ) td(B) .

This is the Riemann–Roch theorem of Borel and Serre [3]. In a Fourier transform between abelian
varieties the Todd classes are one. This gives the usual cohomological Poincaré transform; compare
the calculation in [17, Section 4.2.3]. The second-factor transform in the fixed functor Φ of (4.29)
uses the inverse Poincaré line and the shift [gX ]. It is therefore

                                   F− (α) = (−1)gX (πXb )∗ e−p πX
                                                                ∗            
                                                                  α .                                (6.2)

The factor (−1)gX is the Chern-character sign of the shift.

Lemma 6.1 (The fixed Fourier matrix). For P ⊂ {1, . . . , ℓX }, k = |P |, one has

                                    F− (eP ) = (−1)k(k+3)/2 ϵP,P c fP c .

In particular F− is a rational isomorphism preserving parity.

Proof. In (6.2), only the complementary indices in e−p can contribute to integration on X. Moving
their f -factors past the e-factors gives the coefficient

                                    (−1) gX +(ℓX −k)(ℓX −k+1)/2 ϵP,P c .

The difference of the displayed exponent and k(k + 3)/2 is 2(gX + 1)(gX − k), since ℓX = 2gX ,
and hence is even. Every basis vector goes to a signed complementary basis vector, proving the
remaining assertions.


                                                     57
   For µ(x, y) = (x + y, y) define the graded Künneth maps

  ϕ = (1 ⊗ F− )µ∗ ,      ν = (F− ⊗ 1)µ∗ ,           D = F−−1 ⊗ F− ,          ϕ′ = ϕ(1 ⊗ τ ) = Dν(1 ⊗ τ ). (6.3)

The input of D is written in the order U ⊕ L, and its output in the order L ⊕ U ; all identifications
use the graded shuffle. These are fixed rational isomorphisms, determined only by the oriented
lattice, addition, and p. Equivalently,

This is finite exterior-algebra coefficient extraction in y1 · · · yℓX ; the output variables x, z represent

    We now identify the tensor to which this transform applies. Write Θ = c1,B (H) ∈ Λ2 L for the
principal class of the seed. Both orbit ideals have n = d + 1 disjoint Abel components, so Lemma 5.3
gives their common Betti character

                                              fn = 1 − n2 Θ2 + n3 Θ3 .

Thus the sheaves F1 = IA (H) and F2 = IΣ (H) used in Φ have common character

                                   v = eΘ fd+1 = 1 + Θ − d2 Θ2 − d6 Θ3 .

Fix K = Q(u), where u2 = −d and d ≥ 3 is the integer of the seed construction, and put

                                 λ+ = euΘ ,          λ− = e−uΘ        in (ΛL)K .

Expanding the exponentials in powers of Θ gives

                                  v = 21 (1 + u−1 )λ+ + (1 − u−1 )λ− .
                                                                             
                                                                                                          (6.4)

The two exponential classes are linearly independent: both have constant term 1, and their degree-
two terms are opposite and nonzero. Reversal acts by (−1)j on Θj , so it interchanges λ+ and λ− .
The rational tensor T = v ⊗ τ v therefore lies in the tensor square of their two-dimensional K-span.
Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch gives

                               β := chB Φ(F2 ⊠ F1 ) = ϕ(v ⊗ v) = ϕ′ (T ).                                 (6.5)

To relate this class to the original sheaf, let ι be the ring involution acting by (−1)j on H 2j (M, Q).
The shifts in G = Φ(F2 ⊠ F1 )[−3] and E = DM (G)[−1] give
                                               
                      E = DM Φ(F2 ⊠ F1 ) [2],             chB (E) = ιβ,           β0 = r = 8d.            (6.6)

Here the rank is supplied by Proposition 4.7. We next introduce the Clifford action on ΛL and
compare its transport through ϕ′ with the graded action on H ∗ (M ). The exponential correction
will disappear under the normalization defining κM .

6.2    The exact Clifford identity
Give V the symmetric split form

                             Q(ei , fj ) = δij ,        Q(ei , ej ) = Q(fi , fj ) = 0.

                                                          58
Use the Clifford algebra with relation vw + wv = Q(v, w). We use the standard Clifford construction
of the spin group in this normalization: the norm-one even Clifford normalizer Spin(V, Q) acts on V
by conjugation through ρ : Spin(V, Q) → SO(V, Q), and acts on a Clifford module by multiplication.
The norm is Clifford reversion times the element. For this split rational construction, see [17,
Sections 3.2 and 3.4–3.5]. Only this construction, not a realization of arbitrary spin elements by
derived autoequivalences, is used here.
    On S = ΛL the Fock action is

                                   c(e) = ϵe = e ∧ −,          c(f ) = ιf .

Write mg = c(g). Conjugation by the fixed ϕ′ defines the rational algebraic representation

                                   ρ′g = ϕ′ (mg ⊗ mg )(ϕ′ )−1       on ΛV.

The following calculation is the matrix-unit Clifford realization underlying [17, Proposition 3.2.1(e)],
with the signs of the fixed transform retained.

Proposition 6.2 (Fixed Clifford identity). For every algebraic spin element g, one has the exact
equality of rational representations
                                                                2
                              ρ′g = ep/2 Λρ(g)e−p/2 = e(p−Λ ρ(g)p)/2 Λρ(g).                          (6.7)

The exponentials denote exterior multiplication. In particular

                             [ρ′g (1)]0 = 1,       [ρ′g (1)]2 = 12 p − Λ2 ρ(g)p .
                                                                               


Proof. Exterior multiplication and contraction give every matrix unit between the monomial basis
vectors of S: first project onto a specified monomial by products of the commuting occupation
projections ϵei ιfi and ιfi ϵei , and then remove and insert the required generators. The ordered
Clifford monomials span at most 22ℓX dimensions, which equals dim End(S). Thus

                                               C(V, Q) ≃ End(S).

Its even part is the parity-preserving part: the inclusion follows from parity of the generators, and
both sides have dimension 22ℓX −1 . Put F = f1 · · · fℓX in the Clifford algebra and define

                            φ : S ⊗ S −→ C(V, Q),            φ(s ⊗ t) = sF τ (t).

On S this is the rank-one operator

                                 c(φ(s ⊗ t))(r) = (−1)gX [τ (t) ∧ r]top s.                           (6.8)

Indeed F contracts the full oriented monomial with sign (−1)ℓX (ℓX −1)/2 = (−1)gX . The pairing
⟨t, r⟩ = [τ (t) ∧ r]top is perfect. Exterior multiplication is self-adjoint for it by reversal of products.
Contraction is self-adjoint as well: apply ιf to a zero product in exterior degree ℓX + 1 and use

                                       τ ιf |Λk L = (−1)k−1 ιf τ |Λk L .

Consequently Clifford reversion is adjunction for this pairing. Every spin element has reversion
norm one, and therefore preserves the pairing. Formula (6.8) now proves that φ is an isomorphism
intertwining the diagonal spin action with conjugation on C(V, Q).

6.3   The quadratic action and its actual polarization

                   ϑ : U −→ L,           ϑ(y) = ιy Θ,        ιy (e ∧ e′ ) = y(e)e′ − y(e′ )e.

6.4    The ordered stabilizer and its exterior invariants
The next calculation identifies exactly which invariant part can carry a Weil class. It also distinguishes
fixing the two spinor vectors from merely fixing their lines.

Proposition 6.4 (Ordered spinor stabilizer). Let W = W+ . Under VK = W ⊕ W ∗ , the ordered
line stabilizer and the pointwise stabilizer are
 e K = {(g, t) ∈ GL(W ) × Gm : t2 = det g},
 L                                                   m(g, t) = t−1 Λg,       JK = {(g, 1) : g ∈ SL(W )}.

The characters on λ+ and λ− are t, t−1 . For 0 ≤ j ≤ 6,
                                         j
                                   (
                              J        QθM ,              j ̸= 3,
                    2j
                  H (M, Q) =             3 ⊕ W (M ),
                                                                      H odd (M, Q)J = 0.              (6.17)
                                       QθM    K           j = 3,

                                         j
The ordered line stabilizer fixes every θM and acts on the two scalar Weil lines by t2 , t−2 .

Proof. The two annihilators in (6.12) are complementary maximal isotropic spaces, paired perfectly
by Q. Identify the second with W ∗ . The Clifford algebra on W ⊕ W ∗ acts on ΛW by wedge and
contraction. As in the matrix-unit argument above it is the full matrix algebra on this module.
Hence this module is isomorphic to SK as a Clifford module. The common annihilator of all wedge
operators from W is the top line Kω, while the common annihilator of contractions from W ∗ is the
vacuum line K1. The two original spinor lines therefore correspond to Kω, K1, in that order.
   An ordered stabilizer must preserve the two annihilators, so its vector action is diag(g, g −T ) for
g ∈ GL(W ). Conversely a lift of such a block action preserves both one-dimensional annihilator
spaces. The operator Pg = Λg on ΛW implements this block action by direct conjugation of wedge
and contraction. It is in the even Clifford algebra because it preserves parity. For the top reversal
pairing,
                                      ⟨Pg s, Pg t⟩ = det(g)⟨s, t⟩.
Thus its Clifford reversion norm is Peg Pg = det g. Any other Clifford lift of the same vector action
differs by a scalar: their quotient commutes with all of the full matrix algebra. A norm-one lift is
therefore aPg with a2 det g = 1. Writing t = a−1 proves the asserted algebraic cover and action. The
top and vacuum characters are t−1 det g = t and t−1 . Fixing both vectors is exactly t = 1, hence
exactly SL(W ). In particular the extra covering element (1, −1), which negates both spinors, is not
in JK . This is a statement of algebraic groups; it does not assert that every K-rational determinant
has a square root in K. The map t 7→ (diag(t2 , 1, . . . , 1), t) is an algebraic section of the character t;
in particular t2 , t−2 are distinct nontrivial characters.
    Now
                                                      Λa W ⊗ Λb W ∗ .
                                                 M
                                     Λk (VK ) =
                                                a+b=k

The invariant vectors in a summand are the intertwiners HomSL(W ) (Λb W, Λa W ). Here is the
                                                                                             Q
elementary calculation of those intertwiners. In a basis w1 , . . . , w6 , the diagonal torus ti = 1 acts
on wI with weight i∈I ti . Two such weights, for I, J ⊂ {1, . . . , 6}, agree on this torus exactly
                          Q

when their indicator vectors differ by a constant multiple of (1, . . . , 1). Thus either I = J, or
(I, J) = ({1, . . . , 6}, ∅), or the reverse. For 1 ≤ a ≤ 5, any invariant subspace of Λa W is a sum of
its one-dimensional torus weight spaces. The elementary unipotents wi 7→ wi + cwj connect every
two a-subsets by successive replacements, so a nonzero invariant subspace contains all the weight
spaces. This proves irreducibility. In an endomorphism commuting with the torus the coefficients


                                                     64
on these weight spaces are diagonal, and commuting with the same unipotents makes them all equal.
Consequently the only invariant blocks are

                                   a = b,       (a, b) = (6, 0), (0, 6),

and each is one-dimensional. The balanced identity tensor is the degree-two class i wi ∧ wi∗ ; its j-th
                                                                                       P

power is nonzero and spans the balanced block a = b = j. The already constructed nonzero invariant
θM is a scalar multiple of that class. The other two lines are det W, det W ∗ , with ordered-group
characters det g = t2 and (det g)−1 = t−2 .
     Invariants commute with extension of scalars: they are the kernel of the coaction difference, and
field extension is flat. The group J and θM are rational. The two determinant lines are exchanged
by scalar conjugation and descend to the rational Weil plane. They occupy the exterior summands
(a, b) = (6, 0), (0, 6) in Λa W ⊗ Λb W ∗ , whereas θM
                                                    3 occupies the (a, b) = (3, 3) summand. Thus the

sum in degree six is direct. Descent gives (6.17), including the vanishing in odd degrees.

6.6    The full normalized character is Hodge on the Weil domain
We now relate the vector stabilizer to the actual polarized complex structures. This uses the
polarization matrix itself, so no cohomological Hermitian trace-dual convention is needed.

Proposition 6.6 (The actual polarized Hodge circle). Keep the rational homology of the seed M ,
the action η, and the Riemann form of LM fixed. At every compatible marked complex structure of
Weil type (3, 3), every component (κM )j has Hodge type (j, j).

Proof. Let C be the coefficient matrix of θM on cohomology. It is also the matrix of its alternating
Riemann form on dual homology. Equation (6.14) gives

                                             C = −f Q−1 .

For a cohomology automorphism g commuting with the invertible f , this implies

                            gCg t = C ⇐⇒ −f (gQ−1 g t ) = −f Q−1
                                                                                                  (6.19)
                                       ⇐⇒ gQ−1 g t = Q−1 ⇐⇒ g t Qg = Q.

Thus among K-linear cohomology operators, fixing the actual polarization bivector is exactly
orthogonality for Q.
   Let I be a compatible complex structure on homology and let T = η(u)∗ . Put ht = cos t + sin t I,
and let gt = htt be its cohomology pullback. Since IT = T I and T t = f , one has gt f = f gt .
Compatibility e(Ix, Iy) = e(x, y) says I t CI = C. Since I 2 = −1, it also gives I t C + CI = 0;
expansion of htt Cht then gives htt Cht = C. This is gt Cgtt = C. Equation (6.19) places gt in the
centralizer of f in O(Q), and its path from the identity places it in SO(Q).
   After the usual complex scalar extension of W+ , the two Hodge-circle weights have multiplicities
three and three. Hence
                                     det(gt |W+ ) = e3it e−3it = 1.
Using the inverse circle convention only interchanges the factors. By Proposition 6.4, after extending
to K the determinant-one vector centralizer is SL(W+ ), and its unique lift fixing both spinors is
(g, 1) ∈ JK . This identity of algebraic groups descends from K. The actual polarized Hodge circle is
therefore in the vector image of J.
     On H p,q the circle has weight p − q, up to simultaneous reversal. A degree-2j class fixed by it has
only type (j, j). Proposition 6.5 makes every component of κM J-invariant, proving the assertion at
every compatible marked period.



                                                   66
    Only a constant rational transport will be needed for the quotient q : M → Y0 . Transporting
homology, the K-action, and the polarization by q∗ identifies the marked complex structures; under
these identifications q ∗ is a constant rational cohomology map and respects every corresponding
Hodge decomposition. This observation concerns classes on the marked domain, and requires no
relative isogeny or relative descent of the sheaf.


7     Descent and deformation
We now pass from the equivariant sheaf on the Jacobian product to an ordinary sheaf whose full
obstruction group can be used in deformation. The first step is coherent twisted descent through the
finite quotient and a projective-bundle conversion. The second is the restricted smooth-projective-
germ semiregularity theorem needed for that ordinary sheaf.

7.1     Twisted descent and conversion to an ordinary sheaf
The deformation argument will use an ordinary coherent sheaf on a projective family. We first
construct a twisted sheaf on the quotient of the abelian sixfold and compare its full derived
obstruction group with the invariant obstruction group on the covering sixfold. We then cancel the
twist on a relative projective bundle. Throughout, the group action on an endomorphism complex is
the conjugation action induced by the given line-twisted action; an action on the sheaf by ordinary
automorphisms is not required.

7.1.1    Characters and the duality sign
For a perfect complex P on a smooth complex manifold V , write

                                aP = − AtBF (P ) ∈ Ext1V (P, P ⊗ Ω1V ).

This is the raw first-jet Atiyah class with positive line-bundle Chern sign. The opposite sign is the
convention of Buchweitz–Flenner [4, Definition 3.4 and Definition 4.1]. Use the Atiyah-power and
perfect-trace conventions of Proposition 5.1. Define

The summands with j > dim V − 2 vanish. These definitions also apply to a perfect weight-one

For an ordinary perfect complex this is its rational topological Chern character. For a twisted

On the semiregularity summand H j+2 (ΩjV ), the corresponding normalization is (2πi)−j ιV : its
weight is the form degree j, not half the total cohomological degree. Thus, if ιV (γH ) = (2πi)γB for
γH ∈ H 1 (Ω1V ), multiplication by γH on the raw targets corresponds weightwise to multiplication by
γB . All identities involving σ will remain in raw Hodge cohomology.
    Return to the fixed perfect complex G on M = X × X.    b Write

                         D(G) = RHomM (G, OM ),              E = D(G)[−1] = E.
By Proposition 4.7, E is a coherent perfect sheaf of rank r = 8d > 0. Proposition 4.8 supplies the
coherent line-twisted actions on G and E, both indexed by Gsrc and identified with the translation
subgroup Ḡ through g 7→ bg . Duality inverts the line factors and preserves the translations and
coherent composition law. Proposition 5.8 proves that σG is injective on the full group Ext2M (G, G)Ḡ .
The next calculation transfers this injection to E.
Lemma 7.1. Even transpose induces an isomorphism
                                                      ∼
                                d2 : Ext2M (G, G)Ḡ −−→ Ext2M (E, E)Ḡ .
With the convention (7.1), its components obey
                            σE,j (d2 v) = (−1)j+1 σG,j (v)    for every j ≥ 0.                    (7.3)
Consequently, injectivity of σG on the full Ḡ-invariant Ext2 implies injectivity of σE on the full
Ḡ-invariant Ext2 .
Proof. Perfect duality is an anti-equivalence and turns the given line-twisted action into its inverse-
line action. The quasi-isomorphism from D(G)[−1] to the degree-zero sheaf E is canonical in the
derived category and is equivariant. Transpose therefore gives the asserted invariant isomorphism in
even degree.
    The sign can be computed in a finite complex of free modules. The dual Atiyah class is the
negative transpose of the original class. Transposing a product reverses both its Ext-degree-one
factors and its one-form factors. The two reversal Koszul signs cancel: equivalently, an Ω1 [1]-valued
Atiyah operation has even combined parity. The Ext-degree-two operation v is also even, so its
movement past these factors gives no further sign. Supertrace is invariant under transpose, while
the shift [−1] changes supertrace by −1. The j negative dual Atiyah factors and this last sign give
(7.3). Evaluation, transpose, and the Atiyah construction respect changes of finite free model, so
the local calculation glues. The diagonal operator with entries (−1)j+1 is invertible, which proves
the final assertion.

be the finite etale translation isogeny of Proposition 4.8. We write the action on E using pullback

                                     Lh ⊗ t∗h E ≃ E          (h ∈ Ḡ).
These isomorphisms retain the unital associative line compositors of that proposition. Reindexing
by inversion on Ḡ preserves the invariant subspace of the derived endomorphisms.

Proof. We construct the twisted sheaf before comparing its derived endomorphisms. Choose an
evenly covered good analytic cover of Y0 , refined so that the finitely many transition lines are trivial
on the required sheet intersections. Take every sheet over every cover member, so the sheets Ui

Vi . On Vi ∩ Vj for i < j, the deck transformation comparing the two sheets and a local trivialization

the translations; the remaining line trivializations differ by an invertible scalar function. Thus
ρij ρjk ρki = cijk id.

complex has the alternating tensor product of the determinants of its terms. It commutes with
restriction and isomorphism, and the determinant of c id on a rank-r perfect complex is cr . Each Bi
is perfect, so det Bi is a line. Trivialize these lines and let ηij be the determinant of ρij ; then ηii = 1

                                              ρ′ij ρ′jk = ϵijk ρ′ik .

    For clarity, a twisted sheaf here is a coherent weight-one sheaf on Y0 , equivalently the just
described Cech data. Weight-zero sheaves and complexes descend uniquely to Y0 . A coherent twisted
sheaf on the smooth manifold is absolutely perfect: in a local trivialization of the gerbe it is a finite
module over a regular analytic local ring and has a finite free resolution. Write

                                             CB = RHomY0 (B, B)

for its weight-zero descent. It is a perfect complex on Y0 , locally B ∨ ⊗L B.
    We use the following derived base-change identity. For an absolutely perfect P , any complex Q′ ,
and any morphism f : T → V ,

                             Lf ∗ RHomV (P, Q′ ) ≃ RHomT (Lf ∗ P, Lf ∗ Q′ ).                             (7.7)

Indeed, locally the left side is Lf ∗ (P ∨ ⊗L Q′ ); derived pullback is symmetric monoidal and carries
the dual of a perfect complex to the dual of its pullback. This also proves the identity on gerbe
trivializations. The unit and trace, being coevaluation and evaluation, commute with the same
derived pullbacks. This is an assertion about derived pullback; ordinary pullback under a nonflat
map is not being substituted.
    Apply (7.7) to the etale map q, where ordinary and derived pullback agree. Line cancellation in
(7.4) gives
                             q ∗ CB ≃ RHom(q ∗ B, q ∗ B) ≃ RHomM (E, E).                         (7.8)
Both the line factors and their scalar gluing cancel. The descent datum on the right is consequently
the honest conjugation action of Ḡ, although the action on E itself is line-twisted.
    For a finite covering, q∗ on sheaves is exact: over an evenly covered open set it is the direct sum
of the finitely many sheet restrictions. Etale descent gives CB ≃ (q∗ q ∗ CB )Ḡ . Over C, invariants are
the split summand defined by |Ḡ|−1 h∈Ḡ h. Applying derived global sections to that summand
                                       P

and using exactness of q∗ gives

                                       RΓ(Y0 , CB ) ≃ RΓ(M, q ∗ CB )Ḡ .

Taking cohomology and using (7.8) proves the full Ext comparison in (7.5).

     It remains to compare the semiregularity maps. The cotangent target on a µr -gerbe is that
of its underlying manifold. Equivalently, the scalar triple-gluing functions are locally constant, so
their differentials vanish in first jets. The pullback and tensor rules for Atiyah classes, with the

                                                                   adet E

with the Atiyah operations in the combined Ext/form grading. Expanding the exponential and

which is (7.6). Multiplication by λH ∈ H 1 (Ω1M ) raises both Hodge indices and is nilpotent on
H−2 (M ); e−λH therefore has inverse eλH . Full invariant injectivity on the right implies full injectivity
on the left. The trace-free comparison also shows that injectivity restricted to the invariant trace-free
group would suffice: the j = 0 component of σB is the perfect trace itself, so its vanishing first puts
a class into the trace-free summand.

The right side of the second equality is rational. The Betti trace q∗B satisfies q∗B q ∗ = (deg q) id, so

7.1.3   A relative projective bundle carrying an ordinary sheaf
Let a : Y → S be a holomorphic abelian scheme of relative dimension six over a sufficiently small
contractible representative of a smooth analytic germ, with central fiber Y0 . Assume that the family
is polarized. Locally it admits a line LY representing the polarization and ample relative to a.
More precisely, if only the polarization homomorphism was specified, the standard rigidified relative

                                                     71
Picard component of representing lines is a torsor under the smooth dual abelian scheme; a local
analytic section through a chosen central representative supplies LY . Relative ampleness holds after
shrinking. This is the only local choice of a representing polarization line that will be used.
    We realize the twisting class by a finite projective representation and extend its projective-space
bundle over Y . On the central fiber, the inverse-twisted tautological line cancels the twist of the
pulled-back sheaf B. The vanishing of higher cohomology of the structure sheaf on projective space
retains the full obstruction group in this conversion.

     The multiplication map [r] : Y → Y is a finite etale torsor under Y [r]. Its differential on the

projective-space bundle with these projective transitions.

                                                              ^2

In the bar complex the cycle [ei |ej ] − [ej |ei ] represents ei ∧ ej , and an inflated multiplier evaluates

On a common good cover, its µm -cocycle therefore differs from a chosen µr -cocycle for α by the

their projectivizations give Z0 .

projective, for instance by its closed graph in a projective product. The global line H is p-ample.

centrally it gives aℓ0 = ζH,0 .

7.2    Deformation over a smooth projective analytic germ
The ordinary coherent-sheaf deformation statement below is the projective case of the argument
in Buchweitz–Flenner [4, Theorem 5.1 and its proof, pp. 174–179], whose proof constructs a flat
analytic sheaf with the prescribed central fiber. We reconstruct that argument for the projective
bundle in Proposition 7.3, keeping the full coefficient obstruction group explicit. Projectivity permits
a comparison of the Chern characters of all infinitesimal lifts and an effectivity argument through
compatible quotients.

Theorem 7.4. Let S = (S, 0) be a smooth complex analytic germ, and let π : X → S be a proper
holomorphic submersion of relative dimension n, equipped with a holomorphic π-ample line LX . Let


                                                        74
F0 be an ordinary coherent sheaf on X0 . For I ⊆ {1, . . . , n}, suppose the following raw semiregularity
map is injective:

                                                     H k+1 (X0 , Ωk−1
                                               M
                 σI,0 : Ext2X0 (F0 , F0 ) −→                      X0 ),
                                               k∈I
                                                                                                   (7.19)
                                                                       ak−1
                                                                              !         !!
                                                                        F0
                                       v 7−→    TrF0 ,Ωk−1 [k+1]              [2] ◦ v
                                                         X0          (k − 1)!            k∈I

Suppose there are rational flat sections βB,k of R2k π∗ Q, for k ∈ I, such that

                          βB,k (0) = chB,k (F0 ),        βB,k ∈ F k (R2k π∗ C ⊗ OS ).

The second condition means that the flat de Rham class itself lies in the indicated Hodge filtration.
Then, after shrinking S, there is a coherent S-flat sheaf F on X , with F |X0 ≃ F0 , for which

                               chB,k (F |Xs ) = βB,k (s)      (s near 0, k ∈ I).                   (7.20)

These classes are rational algebraic cycle classes. No rank, simplicity, stability, local-freeness,
reflexivity, or fixed-support hypothesis is imposed on F0 .

    We use the raw convention a = − AtBF from (7.1). With ordinary de Rham differential d, the raw
degree-k Chern character is the Hodge edge of (2πi)k chB,k . Thus the prescribed raw Hodge section
in the theorem is the edge of (2πi)k βB,k . The factor does not change horizontality or membership
in F k .

7.2.1   Absolute perfectness and the coefficient obstruction group
Write
             R = OS,0 = C{t1 , . . . , te },     Aν = R/mν+1 ,            Xν = X ×S Specan Aν .
Here m is the maximal ideal of R. The integer ν ≥ 0 records the infinitesimal order.
    We construct compatible Aν -flat sheaves Eν with central fiber F0 . If Eν has been constructed,
the obstruction to the next lift has coefficients in Jν = ker(Aν+1 → Aν ). The first lemma identifies
its group with Ext2X0 (F0 , F0 ) ⊗C Jν , on which central semiregularity remains injective. For these
extensions, differentiation gives an injection Jν → Ω1Aν+1 /C ⊗Aν+1 Aν . The comparison with Gauss–
Manin then forces the obstruction to vanish if each selected component of the relative Chern
character of Eν is the edge of its prescribed flat filtered class. We prove this character identity for
every infinitesimal lift in Lemma 7.7; thus any lift produced at one stage satisfies the hypothesis
needed at the next. Finally, we express the sheaves as quotients of one vector bundle by lifting the
quotient-defining sections compatibly. The resulting formal section of the relative Quot space admits
an analytic approximation preserving the central quotient. Pulling back the universal quotient gives
a flat sheaf with central fiber F0 , whose fiber characters are determined by flat transport.

Lemma 7.5. Let A = Aν , X = Xν , and let E be an A-flat coherent sheaf on X whose central
fiber is F0 . Then E is absolutely perfect as an OX -complex, with local projective dimension at most
n. If W is a finite A-module killed by the maximal ideal and WX = OX ⊗A W , there are natural
identifications

                                Ext2X (E, E ⊗A W ) ≃ Ext2X0 (F0 , F0 ) ⊗C W,
                                                                                                   (7.21)
                              H q (X, ΩjX/A ⊗A W ) ≃ H q (X0 , ΩjX0 ) ⊗C W.

                                                        75
Under them the coefficient semiregularity map

                                                      aX/A (E)k−1 L
                                                                           !             !!
               σI,W (v) =    TrE,(Ωk−1 ⊗A W )[k+1]               ⊗A 1W         [2] ◦ v
                                   X/A                  (k − 1)!                          k∈I

is exactly σI,0 ⊗ 1W , and is therefore injective.

Proof. At x ∈ X0 , put B = OX,x and ME = Ex . Both are A-flat, while B0 = B ⊗A C is regular local
of dimension n. Choose a minimal finite-rank free B-resolution of ME , initially allowed to be infinite.
Tensoring it over A with C remains exact because its terms and ME are A-flat. It is still minimal
over B0 , since maximal-ideal entries remain in the maximal ideal after reduction. The regular-local
projective-dimension theorem gives projective dimension at most n over B0 , so this minimal reduced
resolution has no terms in higher degree. Its ranks are the ranks before reduction, and the original
resolution therefore stops as well. This proves absolute OX -perfectness. It is separate from relative
Tor-amplitude [0, 0], which is precisely the assumed A-flatness; the nonreduced total space has not
been declared regular. For the general fiberwise perfectness criterion, see [4, Proposition 4.5 and
Lemma 4.6, p. 169].
    Let i : X0 ,→ X. Flatness of OX and E over A, together with derived associativity, gives

        Li∗ E ≃ E ⊗L
                   A C = F0 ,        E ⊗L          L
                                        OX WX ≃ E ⊗A W = E ⊗A W = i∗ (F0 ⊗C W ).                     (7.22)

In particular the last derived tensor is an ordinary sheaf. We do not use the generally false identity
Li∗ WX = OX0 ⊗C W . Perfect duality and the projection formula for the closed immersion give

                 RHomX (E, E ⊗A W ) ≃ E ∨ ⊗L i∗ (F0 ⊗ W ) ≃ i∗ (F0∨ ⊗L F0 ⊗ W ).

Derived global sections prove the first identity in (7.21). The coefficient sheaf ΩjX/A ⊗A W is the
closed pushforward of ΩjX0 ⊗C W , proving the second. These are finite direct-sum identities, not
higher-direct-image base change for an endomorphism complex.
    The relative first-jet Atiyah class pulls back to that of F0 , and evaluation E ∨ ⊗ E → OX pulls
back to central evaluation. Apply these identities to the displayed perfect-Hom formula. Each
coefficient semiregularity component becomes the corresponding central component tensored with
W . This proves the asserted equality and injectivity.

              J = mν+1 /mν+2 ,        K = Ω1A′ /C ⊗A′ A,      j : X = Xν ,→ X ′ = Xν+1 .

Lemma 7.6. Let E be an A-flat coherent sheaf on X with central fiber F0 . Suppose, for every
k ∈ I, that its raw relative character chrel                  k

                k                     ′   ′

prescribed restriction to X.
Proof. We first record the precise coefficient exactness needed for (7.23). The relative ample line
algebraizes every Xν , and every coherent sheaf on it, over the finite complex algebra Aν . We use
projective analytic GAGA in the form of an exact equivalence on coherent sheaves and comparison
on their cohomology; local analytification is faithfully flat [37]. The algebraic model is projective and
smooth over Aν : its analytification is locally a smooth product, and smoothness for these finite-type
models is reflected by analytification. Deligne’s proper-smooth theorem [12, Theorem 5.5] applies in
characteristic zero, including to nonreduced Artinian bases. It states that H q (Xν , ΩjXν /Aν ) is finite
locally free, commutes with arbitrary algebra base change, and that the Hodge-to-de-Rham spectral
sequence degenerates.
    Here is the finite-module consequence. For a finite A-module W , apply algebra base change to
the split square-zero algebra A ⊕ W . On the same underlying space the relative differential sheaf is
ΩjX/A ⊕ (ΩjX/A ⊗A W ). The base-change isomorphism respects the inclusion and augmentation of
A ⊕ W . Taking the W -summand gives, naturally in W ,
                              H q (X, ΩjX/A ⊗A W ) ≃ H q (X, ΩjX/A ) ⊗A W.                          (7.24)
The latter Hodge module is free, so this functor of W is exact. In particular d : J ,→ K stays
injective after (7.24). GAGA transfers the same assertion to analytic sheaves.
    For the remaining derived calculation, algebraize X ′ , reduce it to obtain X, and algebraize E;
use the same symbols for this projective algebraic pair. Faithfully flat local analytification reflects
A-flatness, and the proof of Lemma 7.5 gives absolute perfectness on the algebraic model too. The
derived foundations used here are the usual tensor and adjunction formalism for modules, cotangent
transitivity, the first conormal homology H −1 (LX/X ′ ) = JX /JX 2 , and the functorial derived principal-

parts construction of the Atiyah class. For a perfect complex the trace is evaluation; its cyclicity and
its compatibility with pullback and coefficient maps follow in a finite free model. We also use the
characteristic-zero shifted exterior convention, for a cotangent complex L, L Symk (L[1]) ≃ (L k L)[k].

These are the standard algebraic cotangent and perfect-duality constructions; no obstruction theorem
for a general perfect complex is imported.

7.2.3       The Chern character of every infinitesimal lift
The one-step lemma can be iterated only after checking that a chosen lift has the prescribed relative
character, rather than merely the prescribed central restriction. The projectivity assumption supplies
a direct proof for every stage.
Lemma 7.7. For every Aν -flat coherent sheaf Eν on Xν with central fiber F0 , and every 0 ≤ k ≤ n,
the ordinary flat comparison

Proof. For k = 0 the assertion is constancy of rank on each connected component. Assume k ≥ 1.
The projective model of Xν has an ample line. Repeated Serre global-generation surjections from
finite sums of negative powers of that line resolve Eν . The local projective-dimension bound in
Lemma 7.5 makes the n-th syzygy locally free. GAGA gives a global finite holomorphic vector-bundle
resolution V • → Eν . No compatibility of these resolutions as ν varies is asserted or needed.

through order ν of relative smooth forms define a morphism

Leibniz’s rule makes this compatible with products, and jets commute with the fiber differential.
The jets of the fiberwise type projections give a bigrading on the target; their integrability identities

the ordinary one with coefficients Aν , up to an invertible formal smooth coordinate change. The
coefficientwise Dolbeault lemma therefore makes it a fine resolution of each ΩpXν /Aν , respecting the
form filtration.
    Both total complexes resolve the constant sheaf Aν . For the holomorphic complex, filter by
powers of m; its associated graded complex is Ω•X0 ⊗C gr Aν , to which the holomorphic Poincare
lemma applies. The jet map is the identity on constants. It therefore realizes the ordinary flat de
Rham comparison in the statement.
    A holomorphic vector bundle V on Xν becomes a smooth Aν -bundle under the same jet map, and
is smoothly isomorphic to V0∞ ⊗Aν . Indeed, modulo successive powers of m, the discrepancy between
its transition matrices and the central ones is a Cech one-cocycle with values in smooth sections
of End(V0∞ ) ⊗ ma /ma+1 . This sheaf is fine, so a smooth gauge change removes that discrepancy.
Induction through the finitely many powers of m gives the isomorphism.

Its curvature has no (0, 2)-part. In the Dolbeault resolution the BF commutator [ ¯        ∂, ∇], with its
form coefficient written after the (0, q)-form, maps under exterior multiplication to FD1,1 . The sign
also follows from the first-jet calculation above: for a line with frames ej = ei gij , the BF cocycle is
−d log gij , while the connection matrices differ by d log gij ; the Cech–Dolbeault boundary carries
this cocycle to + ¯∂Ai . Thus a = − AtBF is represented by −FD1,1 . It follows that the closed form

belongs to F k and that its edge is the raw degree-k Atiyah character.

7.2.4   Compatible quotients and analytic effectivity
Proof of Theorem 7.4. Let βH,k,ν be the edge of the restriction of (2πi)k βB,k to Xν /Aν . A flat
section is uniquely determined near the origin by its central value. Thus Lemma 7.7 identifies the
relative character of every Eν with βH,k,ν . Starting with E0 = F0 , the assumption that βB,k lies in
F k provides the flat lift to order ν + 1. Lemma 7.6 then gives Eν+1 with its identification on Xν .

                                                   81
This constructs a compatible tower of flat coherent sheaves. Only the extensions Aν+1 → Aν and
their modules Jν were used, not arbitrary Artinian extensions.
    We make the classifying maps compatible before applying analytic approximation. Choose c
large enough that F0 (c) = F0 ⊗ LcX ,0 is generated by b global sections and has H 1 (X0 , F0 (c)) = 0.
Let V = (L−c X )
                ⊕b and fix the central quotient V ↠ F . Given a quotient V ↠ E , tensor the
                                                    0      0                       ν      ν
extension for Eν+1 by LcX . Its kernel is i0∗ (F0 (c) ⊗C Jν ), whose first cohomology vanishes. The
global-section map to order ν is therefore surjective. Lift the b sections defining the quotient. Their
map to Eν+1 is surjective by Nakayama’s lemma, because its central restriction is the fixed central
quotient. We obtain compatible quotients Vν ↠ Eν for every ν.
    We use the relative analytic Quot theorem of Pourcin [32, Theorem 2, pp. 451–452]: proper
flat quotients of a coherent sheaf on a separated finite-dimensional complex space over a separated
finite-dimensional complex base, which may be nonreduced, are represented by a complex space
with a universal flat quotient. These hypotheses hold here; in particular quotient supports are
proper because X → S is proper. Let T be the germ of this Quot space at the central quotient. The
compatible quotients give unique compatible S-maps Specan Aν → T . Passing to the inverse limit
gives a local R-algebra map
                                     OT,0 −→ R b = C[[t1 , . . . , te ]],

that is, a formal section of T → S.
   A graph embedding presents this analytic S-germ as

                                 OT,0 = R{z1 , . . . , zh }/(g1 , . . . , gv ).

The formal section gives zero-constant formal series zj (t) solving these finitely many convergent
equations. Artin’s analytic approximation theorem [1, Theorem 1.2, p. 277] says that such a formal
solution over C has an exact convergent solution with any prescribed finite-order congruence. Take
congruence modulo m. The solution still maps the origin to the central quotient, and, with t fixed
as the parameters, defines an analytic section S → T . Pullback of the universal quotient gives a
coherent S-flat sheaf F with central fiber F0 . The convergent solution need not realize the entire
chosen formal tower; retaining the central fiber is sufficient.
    It remains to identify characters on actual fibers. The total space X is smooth, so F is absolutely
perfect. Separately, S-flatness and derived associativity give, for every s,

                      Li∗s F ≃ F ⊗L       L             L
                                  OX (OX ⊗OS k(s)) ≃ F ⊗OS k(s) = F |Xs [0].                     (7.34)

Near X0 , F has a finite resolution by total holomorphic vector bundles: use relative Serre generation
repeatedly and the regular-local projective-dimension bound on the smooth total space, then shrink
by properness at the final locally free syzygy. The alternating normalized topological Chern character
of these bundles restricts by (7.34) to chB (F |Xs ). Restrictions of a total cohomology class along a
proper smooth submersion are flat. Their central value is chB (F0 ), so uniqueness of flat transport
proves (7.20).
    Every Xs is projective algebraic, and projective GAGA algebraizes F |Xs . On the smooth
projective fiber a finite locally free resolution identifies its normalized topological character with
the cycle class of its rational algebraic Chern character. Its raw degree-k Atiyah character is the
weightwise (2πi)k image of that class. This proves algebraicity and completes the theorem.

   For p : Z → Y in Proposition 7.3, the morphism a ◦ p satisfies the theorem’s projectivity
hypothesis, F0 is ordinary coherent and fully semiregular, and its relative dimension is N = 6 + m − 1.
Taking I = {1, . . . , N }, the raw input in degree k is exactly the edge of (2πi)k AB,k . The theorem
uses neither a relative version of the finite isogeny q nor a deformation of the twisted sheaf B. Its

                                                      82
proof also does not assert arbitrary classical base change for global Ext groups or higher direct
images. The smooth projective germ and the ordinary coherent-sheaf condition are the stated limits
of the deformation result.


8     Algebraicity at every compatible period
We apply the descent and deformation results to the normalized character computed for the polarized
seed. Projective-bundle operations first give local algebraicity. A marked period family and proper
Hilbert parameter spaces then extend the Weil plane to every compatible period, and rational
isogenies remove the dependence on the initial lattice.

8.1.1    The actual homological Hermitian space
Let eM be the Riemann form of the ample class θM . The actual homological Hermitian form is

                               hM (x, y) = eM (x, ηM (u)∗ y) + ueM (x, y).

In the second-linear convention of (3.1), Proposition 6.3 proves that this form is hyperbolic of
signature (3, 3); its three hyperbolic blocks are computed in (6.16).
    Let q : M −→ Y0 be the isogeny in Proposition 7.2. Put

                      V0 = H1 (Y0 , Q),        Λ0 = H1 (Y0 , Z),         θ0 = (q ∗ )−1 θM .

Transport the rational action through q in the category up to isogeny, and write T0 = η0 (u)∗ for the
transported action on V0 . The alternating form e0 associated to θ0 is characterized by

                                          e0 (q∗ x, q∗ y) = eM (x, y).

It is rational and is a positive Riemann form at Y0 ; a positive integer multiple is integral on Λ0 and
is an ample polarization. The second-linear Hermitian form

                                   h0 (x, y) = e0 (x, T0 y) + ue0 (x, y)                          (8.3)

is carried to hM by q∗ , so it too is hyperbolic of signature (3, 3).

                                                      83
8.1.2     The full compatible domain
Let D be the set of real complex structures I on V0,R satisfying

            I 2 = −1,        IT0 = T0 I,        e0 (Ix, Iy) = e0 (x, y),          e0 (x, Ix) > 0    (x ̸= 0).      (8.4)

This is the full marked compatible domain for the fixed rational space, form, and lattice. We verify
both its connectedness and the complex structure used below.    √
     Choose the embedding σ : K ,→ C for which σ(u) = i d, and view V0,σ = V0 ⊗K,σ C with its
Hermitian form h0,σ . A real I commuting with K is complex-linear on this space. Commutation
with T0 together with preservation of e0 implies that I√is unitary for h0,σ . Its i- and −i-eigenspaces
                                                                                             √
are therefore orthogonal. On the first of them T0 = d I, and on the second T0 = − d I. Since
h0 (x, x) = e0 (x, T0 x), Riemann positivity makes the first eigenspace positive and the second negative.
Each has dimension three because h0 has signature (3, 3).
     Conversely, if P ⊂ V0,σ is a positive three-plane, then P ⊥ is negative of dimension three. Set
I = i on P and I = −i on P ⊥ . This is a real complex structure commuting with K and preserving
h0 , hence also e0 , which is the coefficient of u in (8.3). For x = x+ + x− in this orthogonal
decomposition,

                       e0 (x, Ix) = d−1/2 h0,σ (x+ , x+ ) − h0,σ (x− , x− ) > 0
                                                                              
                                                                                        (x ̸= 0).

Thus positive three-planes parameterize exactly D. Fix a positive/negative orthogonal splitting
V0,σ = V+ ⊕ V− . Projection of a positive three-plane to V+ is an isomorphism, so that plane is the
graph of a unique map Z : V+ → V− . Positivity is precisely

                                1 − Z ∗ Z > 0,        equivalently         ∥Z∥op < 1.                              (8.5)

The operator-norm unit ball in HomC (V+ , V− ) is convex. In particular the underlying domain is
connected and contractible and has complex dimension nine.
    For the holomorphic convention, use cohomological periods. Write Wσ ⊕ Wσ̄ = H 1 (Y0 , C)
for the two K-eigenspaces. The cohomological polarization induced by e0 pairs these two spaces
perfectly and vanishes on each separately. If F 1 is a compatible Lagrangian Hodge subspace, then
P ∨ = F 1 ∩ Wσ is a three-plane and

                                       F 1 = P ∨ ⊕ Ann(P ∨ ) ⊂ Wσ ⊕ Wσ̄ ,                                          (8.6)

where the annihilator is taken using that perfect pairing. Conversely this formula, restricted by the
open Riemann positivity condition, gives precisely the compatible periods. The annihilator map
between the two Grassmannians is algebraic. We give D the complex structure in the coordinate P ∨ ;
then (8.6) is holomorphic into the Siegel period domain. This coordinate can be the conjugate of the
homological graph coordinate in (8.5); the connected underlying space and its complex dimension
are unchanged.

8.1.3     A fine principal-level family and the seed lattice
Choose a rational symplectic basis a1 , . . . , a6 , b1 , . . . , b6 for e0 , normalized by e0 (ai , bj ) = δij , and let
Λpp be its integral span. Such a basis follows by repeatedly choosing a pair of pairing one and taking
its symplectic orthogonal complement. The restriction of e0 to Λpp is integral and unimodular. The
Riemann criterion for polarized tori therefore makes

                                        App
                                         I = (V0,R , I)/Λpp ,           I ∈ D,                                     (8.7)

                                                           84
principally polarized abelian sixfolds. The rational K-action persists in End0 (AppI ): every rational
K-operator has an integer multiple preserving Λpp , and it commutes with I.
    We use the following precise fine-level theorem. Over C, fix a primitive fourth root for the
symplectic Weil pairing. The functor of principally polarized abelian schemes of relative dimension
six with a full symplectic level-4 isomorphism to the standard (Z/4)6 × µ64 has a smooth quasi-
projective fine moduli scheme A6 (4) and a universal abelian scheme π : U → A6 (4). On the chosen
analytic component it is the quotient of the universal marked torus family on H6 by the principal
congruence subgroup Γ6 (4); both this component and the universal total space are quasi-projective
algebraic varieties. These are Chai’s symplectic-level and uniformization statements [9, Chapter I,
Theorems 1.4 and 1.7.2, and Sections 2.3–2.5, pp. 5–13]. The hypotheses here are explicit: e0 is
perfect on Λpp , the chosen basis supplies the level, 4 ≥ 3 removes stabilizers, and the ground field
has characteristic zero.
    The universal π is smooth and projective. Smoothness and properness are part of being the
universal abelian scheme. To see projectivity from the stated theorem without choosing a universal
theta line, embed the quasi-projective U locally closed in some PbC . Its graph over the separated
scheme A6 (4) is a locally closed immersion

                                              U −→ PbA6 (4) .

This morphism is proper: for any morphism from an A6 (4)-proper scheme to an A6 (4)-separated
scheme, the graph is closed and the second projection is a base change of a proper morphism. A
proper immersion is a closed immersion. The displayed map is therefore a projective embedding
over A6 (4).
   The symplectic basis identifies the space of all positive Lagrangians with H6 . The compatible
Lagrangians in (8.6) give a holomorphic map D → H6 . Composition with the level quotient gives

                                           ν : D −→ A6 (4)an .                                        (8.8)

The quoted marked uniformization identifies ν ∗ U an with the family (8.7). This construction uses
the ordinary principally polarized level family. It asserts neither that D or its image is algebraic nor
that the ambient universal family has a K-action.

a finite étale holomorphic cover of D, and the marking identifies it with the constant torsion lattice.
The chosen torsion classes therefore select sections, which are holomorphic because the cover is
locally biholomorphic. Translation by Glat is free. Taking the quotient, locally by disjoint translated

because it is the finite quotient of a proper family. Its fiber is (V0,R , I)/(c−1

                m = r12 ,   N = 6 + m − 1,      p : Z → Y,    F0 = p∗0 B ⊗ ℓ0 .

8.2.1    Two algebraic generators of the rational plane
Lemma 8.2. Let A be an abelian sixfold with a unital action of the imaginary quadratic field K. If
a nonzero vector of WK (A) is a rational algebraic class, then every vector of WK (A) is a rational
algebraic class.

Proof. Choose k = 1 + tu ∈ K × with t ∈ Q such that σ(k)6 ̸= σ̄(k)6 . Such t exists because

                                          (1 + tu)6 − (1 − tu)6

is a nonzero polynomial in t, with linear coefficient 12u; only finitely many rational t are excluded.
Over K (or Q) the plane is the direct sum of the two conjugate sixth exterior eigenspaces from the
definition of WK (A). A nonzero rational vector w has nonzero coordinates in both lines: scalar
conjugation exchanges them, so one zero coordinate would force the other to be zero. On the two
lines, k ∗ = η(k)∗ has respective eigenvalues σ(k)6 and σ̄(k)6 . The determinant formed by w and
k ∗ w in those coordinates is therefore nonzero. They are linearly independent over Q, and form a
basis of WK (A).
     Choose an integer nk > 0 clearing the denominator of η(k) ∈ End0 (A). The resulting h = nk η(k)
is an algebraic endomorphism, and on H 6 one has k ∗ = n−6       ∗
                                                             k h . Pullback by h preserves rational
                             ∗
algebraic cycle classes, so k w is algebraic when w is. Both basis vectors, hence the whole rational
plane, are algebraic.

   On the open supplied by Proposition 8.1, the class

                                          wI = κB,3 (I) − cθ θI3

is rational algebraic: an integer multiple of θI is an ample polarization class. It is nonzero everywhere
because it is the transport of the single nonzero marked vector w0 . Lemma 8.2 makes WK (YI )
algebraic on that open. The isogeny (8.10) pulls this plane isomorphically to WK (App     I ): its pullback
on H 1 is a K-linear rational isomorphism and thus identifies both sixth exterior eigenspaces.
Algebraic pullback preserves their cycle classes.
    Let
                                            Wpp ⊂ ν ∗ R6 π∗an Q
be the constant rank-two rational local subsystem defined by the fixed marked K-eigenspaces; its
fiber is WK (App
              I ). We have proved that all its fibers are algebraic on a nonempty analytic open of
D. Only this subsystem, not the individual character κB , will be used in the global step.

8.3     Spreading the plane in the fine family
Proposition 8.3 (Analytic Hilbert spread). Let π : X → S be a smooth projective morphism of
relative dimension n over a smooth quasi-projective complex variety. Let ν : T → S an be holomorphic,
where T is a connected complex manifold. Fix 0 ≤ c ≤ n and a finite-rank rational local subsystem

                                            V ⊂ ν ∗ R2c π∗an Q.

If every vector of Vt is a rational algebraic codimension-c class on Xν(t) for all t in a nonempty
analytic open subset of T , then the same is true for every t ∈ T .

Proof. The assertion is immediate if the rank b of V is zero, so assume b > 0. Fix a relatively very
ample line for X /S. For each of b candidate generators choose a finite list of rational coefficients
and, for each term in that list, a Hilbert polynomial of degree n − c. There are only countably

                                                    88
many such finite choices. For one choice let H be the product over S of the corresponding relative
Hilbert schemes, with structure map ρ : H → S.
    The exact representability result used here is Grothendieck’s fixed-polynomial Hilbert theorem:
for a projective scheme over a noetherian base, a fixed relatively very ample line and Hilbert
polynomial P give a projective relative Hilbert scheme representing closed subschemes flat over
a locally noetherian test scheme with fiber polynomial P , with its universal flat subscheme. It is
the case F = OX of the fixed-polynomial Quot theorem [19, Section 3, Theorem 3.2, pp. 259–260,
and the Hilbert specialization on p. 265]. Here S is noetherian and π is projective, so the theorem
applies. In particular H → S is projective. The space H may be singular or nonreduced; we do
not discard any of its fibers.
    After analytic base change to T , the parameters whose b rational combinations span V will
form an open and closed locus. Properness will make its image a closed analytic subset of T . The
countably many choices cover the assumed open algebraicity locus, so Baire’s theorem and the
identity principle will force one image to be all of T . The first step is to prove the cycle-class
constancy that makes the spanning condition locally constant, including at singular parameter
points.
    Put XH = X ×S H . For each factor of H , let F be the structure sheaf of its pulled-back
universal subscheme on XH . It is coherent and flat over H . It is also perfect on XH , with a
uniform local projective-dimension bound n. To see this, at x 7→ h write

                  R = OH ,h ,      B = OXH ,x ,         M = Fx ,    B = B ⊗R k(h).

Both B and M are R-flat. The local fiber ring B is regular of dimension at most n, since XH → H
is smooth of relative dimension n. Derived associativity and this flatness give

                                M ⊗L                     L
                                   B k(x) ≃ (M ⊗R k(h)) ⊗B k(x).                                  (8.17)

A regular local ring of dimension e has global dimension at most e, as in the regular-local theorem
[39, Tag 00O7, Proposition 10.110.1]. The right side of (8.17) has no Tor homology above n. Over
the noetherian local ring B, build a minimal resolution of the finite module M by choosing minimal
finite generators of each successive kernel. All its differentials become zero after tensoring with k(x).
The vanishing of TorB  n+1 (M, k(x)) therefore forces its (n + 1)-st free term to vanish. This proves
pdB M ≤ n.
    The scheme XH is quasi-projective over C: both H → S and XH → H are projective and S
is quasi-projective. Choose an ample line on XH . The ample-line resolution property says that on
a quasi-compact scheme with such a line every finite-type quasi-coherent sheaf is a quotient of a
finite direct sum of negative powers of the line [39, Tag 01Q3, Proposition 28.27.13(8)]. Successively
take these vector-bundle surjections onto F and its coherent kernels. The n-th syzygy is locally free
by the local projective-dimension bound just proved (and if n = 0, F itself is locally free). Thus F
has a finite global vector-bundle resolution on XH . Derived restriction of that resolution to h is an
ordinary resolution of Fh : its terms are H -flat because XH is H -flat, and F is H -flat, so derived
tensoring with k(h) introduces no higher Tor.
     Here is a topological justification of local constancy that does not require a smooth Hilbert
space. The original π an : X an → S an is a proper smooth submersion between manifolds, because S
and π are smooth. Ehresmann’s fibration theorem gives a differentiable product over a sufficiently
small coordinate ball B around s0 : Xsan0
                                           × B ≃ π −1 (B). Pull back this trivialization of the original
smooth family to H an ; smoothness of the Hilbert space is not required.
     Under this product, restrict one vector bundle in the global resolution to F × V , where F = Xsan 0
is compact and V is a neighborhood of a chosen h0 ∈ H an over B. Shrinking V , finitely many

                                                   89
product neighborhoods Ui × V trivialize the bundle and cover F × V . A partition of unity on F
subordinate to the Ui , multiplied into these trivializations, embeds the bundle continuously in a
fixed trivial complex vector bundle on F × V . Let P (x, h) be the orthogonal projection onto its
image; it is continuous. Compactness of F permits a further shrinking such that

                              ∥P (x, h) − P (x, h0 )∥ < 1     (x ∈ F, h ∈ V ).

The map P (x, h) from im P (x, h0 ) to im P (x, h) is then injective, since a nonzero vector in its kernel
would contradict this inequality; the ranks agree, so it is an isomorphism. These maps are continuous
bundle isomorphisms. Thus the topological Chern character of the bundle on each fiber is locally
constant in h. Apply this to all the finitely many terms of the resolution. Their alternating Chern
character is chB (Fh ) by the exact fiber restriction above. It defines a locally constant section of the
cohomology local system pulled back to H an . This argument is valid at singular and nonreduced
parameter points, since their underlying topology and the restricted topological bundles are all that
was used.
     To identify the cycle class including nonreduced multiplicities, use Baum–Fulton–MacPherson’s
map τ : K0 → CH∗ (−)Q for quasi-projective schemes over a field. It preserves the support-dimension
filtration and sends [OV ] on the associated graded to [V ]; on smooth X, it sends a vector bundle E
to chCH (E) td(TX ) ∩ [X] [2, Chapter II, Theorem, pp. 119–120; Chapter III, Section 1, Theorem
and Lemma, pp. 128–129]. Put s = ρ(h). For Fh supported in dimension at most n − c, let Vi be its
dimension-(n − c) components with generic points ηi , and put ℓi = lengthOXs ,η (Fh,ηi ). Devissage
                                                                                     i
gives                     X
                  [Fh ] ≡    ℓi [OVi ] (mod classes supported in dimension < n − c).
                          i

Indeed, generic composition series spread to dense opens; their discrepancies are lower-dimensional.
Thus τ (Fh ) has no term above dimension n − c and has top term i ℓi [Vi ]. Additivity on a
                                                                        P

vector-bundle resolution gives

                                    τ (Fh ) = chCH (Fh ) td(TXs ) ∩ [Xs ].

Since td0 = 1, comparison in successive codimensions gives chCH,j = 0 for j < c and chCH,c (Fh ) ∩
       P
[Xs ] = i ℓi [Vi ]. Compatibility of the cycle map with normalized Betti Chern classes [2, Section 0.8,
p. 106] therefore gives, for the universal fiber subscheme Zh ,
                                                                    
                                       chB,c (OZh ) = clB [Zh ]n−c .                               (8.18)

Here [Zh ]n−c is the fundamental cycle with its generic scheme lengths. Lower-dimensional and
embedded contributions affect only higher character terms. The preceding local constancy of the
character therefore proves precisely the local constancy of these rational cycle classes on H an .
   Now form the analytic base change

                                        HT = H an ×S an T −→ T.

It is proper and holomorphic: a projective embedding of H over S realizes this base change as a
closed analytic subspace of T × Pa , whose projection is proper. The fixed rational combinations of
the universal classes in (8.18) give locally constant sections of the pulled-back cohomology local
system. In local trivializations the condition that these b sections lie in and span V is a condition
on fixed rational vectors. It is locally constant. Its good locus is open and closed in HT , hence is a
closed analytic subspace and remains proper over T .


                                                     90
    We use Remmert’s proper-image theorem in its reduced-space form: the image of a proper
holomorphic map between reduced complex spaces is an analytic subset. Grauert gives this precise
statement and its derivation from the coherent direct-image theorem in Section 7, Satz 1, p. 60 [18].
Reduce the good locus before applying it. Reduction does not change its underlying proper map or
its image, while T is already reduced. The theorem thus applies even though the original Hilbert
space could be nonreduced or singular. The image of the good locus is a closed analytic subset of T ,
closed also by properness.
    The countably many choices of coefficients and polynomials give countably many such good
images. Their union is exactly the locus where Vt is algebraic. In one direction, a good point
supplies b rational algebraic classes spanning the fiber. Conversely, if that fiber is algebraic, choose a
rational basis and express each basis vector as a finite rational combination of integral codimension-c
subvarieties. Their reduced subschemes have Hilbert polynomials of degree n − c and give a good
point for one of the choices.
    A closed analytic subset unequal to the connected manifold T is nowhere dense by the analytic
identity principle. The assumed nonempty open subset of T is itself a Baire space, so its countable
covering by the good images cannot consist entirely of such nowhere dense subsets. Hence one good
image equals T , which proves the assertion.

   Apply Proposition 8.3 with

                   S = A6 (4),    X =U,      T = D,      n = 6,       c = 3,   V = Wpp .

The family is smooth projective, D is a connected complex manifold, and the local algebraicity
hypothesis was proved above. Therefore
                                                    cl
                     WK (App         3  pp     B
                                                 H 6 (App                      (I ∈ D).
                                                                        
                          I ) ⊂ im CH (AI )Q −
                                             −→        I , Q)                                      (8.19)

The proof applies to the analytically pulled-back rational subsystem. It does not require an algebraic
Weil locus or an algebraic K-action on the ambient fine family.

8.4   Every hyperbolic rational lattice
Proposition 8.4. For the fixed K = Q(u) with u2 = −d, d ≥ 3, let A be any complex abelian
sixfold with a unital K-action and a compatible polarization. If its actual homological Hermitian
form hA (x, y) = eA (x, uy) + ueA (x, y) is hyperbolic, then WK (A) is a rational algebraic plane.

Proof. The hyperbolic-basis assertion of Lemma 3.2 identifies every nondegenerate rank-six hyper-
bolic Hermitian space over K, in the second-linear convention, with three planes of Gram matrix
( 01 10 ). That elementary assertion was proved by splitting isotropic pairs, independently of the
Weil-plane theorem. Since both hA and the actual seed form h0 are hyperbolic, it gives a K-isometry

                                   fA : (H1 (A, Q), hA ) −→ (V0 , h0 ).                            (8.20)

    Let ΛA = H1 (A, Z) and let IA be the complex structure of A. Transport it to I = fA IA fA−1 on
V0,R . The isometry is K-linear, so I commutes with T0 . The Riemann form is recovered without
any dual convention: it is exactly the coefficient of u when h(x, y) = e(x, uy) + ue(x, y) is expanded
in the Q-basis 1, u of K. Thus (8.20) implies

                                       e0 (fA x, fA y) = eA (x, y).



                                                   91
It follows that I preserves e0 and satisfies e0 (x, Ix) > 0 for x ̸= 0. Hence I is a point of the full
connected domain D. Equation (8.19) algebraizes the Weil plane of App    I at this particular period.
    The full rational lattices fA (ΛA ) and Λpp in V0 are commensurable. Choose an integer nA > 0
such that nA fA (ΛA ) ⊂ Λpp . The invertible complex-linear map nA fA then induces a surjective
holomorphic homomorphism with finite kernel

                        φ : A = (H1 (A, R), IA )/ΛA −→ App
                                                        I = (V0,R , I)/Λpp .                    (8.21)

Both tori are projective, so this homomorphism is algebraic by Serre’s Proposition 15 cited above.
It is a K-linear isogeny in the category up to isogeny. Its pullback on first rational cohomology is
therefore a K-linear isomorphism. It maps each of the two embedding eigenspaces isomorphically,
and taking their sixth exterior powers gives

                                      φ∗ WK (App
                                              I ) = WK (A).

Pullback along the algebraic isogeny preserves rational codimension-three cycle classes. The left
side is algebraic by (8.19), proving the proposition.
    The isogeny need not identify the integral polarizations: its pullback scales the rational Riemann
form by n2A . Nor was a lift of fA to a rational spin group used. Only the elementary hyperbolic-
basis argument and a lattice denominator were required, so extra endomorphisms or a product
decomposition of A impose no restriction.

Proof of Theorem 3.1. Every imaginary quadratic field can be written K = Q(u0 ), with u20 = −d0
for a positive squarefree integer d0 . Set u = 2u0 and d = 4d0 ≥ 4. This is the same field and is a
parameter in the range of the seed construction. For any compatible Riemann form,

                            hu (x, y) = e(x, uy) + ue(x, y) = 2hu0 (x, y).

More generally, changing a purely imaginary generator by any nonzero rational factor scales this
Hermitian form by that factor. Such scaling does not change its isotropic subspaces; in signature
(3, 3) it does not change the signature either. Thus the hyperbolic hypothesis of the theorem is
the hyperbolic hypothesis for the generator u used by this seed. Proposition 8.4 applies and gives
the stated algebraicity of WK (A). The full domain in (8.4) and the lattice-clearing isogeny include
every compatible period, with no simplicity or endomorphism-generality assumption.
